# Arthur Schopenhauer

German philosopher (1788–1860)

"Schopenhauer" redirects here. For other uses, see [Schopenhauer (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schopenhauer_\(disambiguation\) "Schopenhauer (disambiguation)").

**Arthur Schopenhauer** ([/ˈʃoʊpənhaʊər/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") [*SHOH-pən-how-ər*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key "Help:Pronunciation respelling key");[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-9) German: [\[ˈaʁtuːɐ̯ ˈʃoːpn̩haʊɐ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Standard_German "Help:IPA/Standard German") [](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/79/De-Arthur_Schopenhauer2.ogg/De-Arthur_Schopenhauer2.ogg.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:De-Arthur_Schopenhauer2.ogg "File:De-Arthur Schopenhauer2.ogg"); 22 February 1788 – 21 September 1860) was a German philosopher. He is known for his 1818 work *[The World as Will and Representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_as_Will_and_Representation "The World as Will and Representation")* (expanded in 1844), which characterizes the [phenomenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenomenon "Phenomenon") world as the manifestation of a blind and irrational [noumenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noumenon "Noumenon") will.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10)[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11)[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-12) Building on the [transcendental idealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcendental_idealism "Transcendental idealism") of [Immanuel Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant "Immanuel Kant"), Schopenhauer developed an [atheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheistic "Atheistic") metaphysical and ethical system that rejected the contemporaneous ideas of [German idealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_idealism "German idealism").[\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-WWR3-7)[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Jacquette-8)

Schopenhauer was among the first philosophers in the [Western tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_philosophy "Western philosophy") to share and affirm significant tenets of [Indian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_philosophy "Indian philosophy"), such as [asceticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asceticism "Asceticism"), denial of the [self](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self_\(philosophy\) "Self (philosophy)"), and the notion of the [world-as-appearance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_\(religion\) "Maya (religion)").[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-13) His work has been described as an exemplary manifestation of [philosophical pessimism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_pessimism "Philosophical pessimism").[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-14) Though his work failed to garner substantial attention during his lifetime, he had a posthumous impact across various disciplines, including philosophy, literature, and science. His writing on [aesthetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schopenhauer%27s_aesthetics "Schopenhauer's aesthetics"), [morality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morality "Morality"), and [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology "Psychology") has influenced many thinkers and artists.

## Early life

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Early life")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Gdansk_Schopenhauer_House.jpg/170px-Gdansk_Schopenhauer_House.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gdansk_Schopenhauer_House.jpg)

Schopenhauer's birthplace house, Heilig-Geist-Gasse

Arthur Schopenhauer was born on 22 February 1788, in [Gdańsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gda%C5%84sk "Gdańsk") (then part of the [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth "Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth"); later in the [Kingdom of Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Prussia "Kingdom of Prussia") *Danzig*) on Św. Ducha 47 (in Prussia Heiliggeistgasse), the son of [Heinrich Floris Schopenhauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Heinrich_Floris_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Heinrich Floris Schopenhauer (page does not exist)") \[[de](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_Floris_Schopenhauer "de:Heinrich Floris Schopenhauer")] and his wife [Johanna Schopenhauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johanna_Schopenhauer "Johanna Schopenhauer") (née Trosiener),[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Google_Books-15) both descendants of wealthy German [patrician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrician_\(post-Roman_Europe\) "Patrician (post-Roman Europe)") families. While they came from a [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism "Protestantism") background, neither of them was very religious;[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 79 [\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Bullock_1920_p._53-17) both supported the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution "French Revolution"),[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 13  were [republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism "Republicanism"), [cosmopolitans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmopolitanism "Cosmopolitanism") and [Anglophiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglophilia "Anglophilia").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 9  When Gdańsk became part of [Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Prussia "Kingdom of Prussia") in 1793, Heinrich moved to [Hamburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburg "Hamburg")—a free city with a republican constitution. His firm continued trading in Danzig where most of their extended families remained. [Adele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adele_Schopenhauer "Adele Schopenhauer"), Arthur's only sibling, was born on 12 July 1797.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

In 1797, Arthur was sent to [Le Havre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Havre "Le Havre") to live with the family of his father's business associate, Grégoire de Blésimaire. He seemed to enjoy his two-year stay there, learning to speak French and fostering a life-long friendship with Jean Anthime Grégoire de Blésimaire.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 18  As early as 1799, Arthur started playing the flute.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 30 

In 1803, he accompanied his parents on a European tour of [Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands "Netherlands"), Britain, France, [Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland "Switzerland"), [Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria "Austria") and [Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prussia "Prussia"). Viewed as primarily a pleasure tour, Heinrich used the opportunity to visit some of his business associates abroad.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

Heinrich presented Arthur with a choice: he could either stay at home to begin preparations for university or travel with them to further his merchant education. Arthur chose to travel with them. He deeply regretted his choice later because the merchant training was very tedious. He spent twelve weeks of the tour attending school in [Wimbledon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wimbledon,_London "Wimbledon, London"), where he was confused by strict and intellectual [Anglicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism "Anglicanism") who he described as shallow. He continued to sharply criticize Anglican religiosity later in life despite his general Anglophilia.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 56  He was also under pressure from his father, who became very critical of his educational results.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

In 1805, Heinrich drowned in a canal near their home in Hamburg. Although it was possible that his death was accidental, his wife and son believed that it was suicide. He was prone to [anxiety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anxiety "Anxiety") and [depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_depressive_disorder "Major depressive disorder"), each becoming more pronounced later in his life.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18) Heinrich had become so fussy, even his wife started to doubt his mental health.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 43  "There was, in the father's life, some dark and vague source of fear which later made him hurl himself to his death from the attic of his house in Hamburg."[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 88 

Arthur showed similar moodiness during his youth and often acknowledged that he inherited it from his father. There were other instances of serious mental health problems on his father's side of the family.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 4  Despite his hardship, Schopenhauer liked his father and later referred to him in a positive light.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 90  Heinrich Schopenhauer left the family with a significant inheritance that was split in three among Johanna and the children. Arthur Schopenhauer was entitled to control of his part when he reached the age of majority. He invested it conservatively in government bonds and earned annual interest that was more than double the salary of a university professor.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 136  After quitting his merchant apprenticeship, with some encouragement from his mother, he dedicated himself to studies at the [Ernestine Gymnasium, Gotha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernestine_Gymnasium,_Gotha "Ernestine Gymnasium, Gotha"), in [Saxe-Gotha-Altenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxe-Gotha-Altenburg "Saxe-Gotha-Altenburg"). While there, he also enjoyed social life among the local nobility, spending large amounts of money, which deeply concerned his frugal mother.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 128  He left the Gymnasium after writing a satirical poem about one of the schoolmasters. Although Arthur claimed that he left voluntarily, his mother's letter indicates that he may have been expelled.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 129 

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/ArthurSchopenhauer.jpg/220px-ArthurSchopenhauer.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ArthurSchopenhauer.jpg)

Schopenhauer in his youth

Arthur spent two years as a merchant in honor of his dead father. During this time, he had doubts about being able to start a new life as a scholar.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 120  Most of his prior education was as a practical merchant and he had trouble learning Latin; a prerequisite for an academic career.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 117 

His mother moved away, with her daughter Adele, to [Weimar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar "Weimar")—then the centre of [German literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_literature "German literature")—to enjoy social life among writers and artists. Arthur and his mother did not part on good terms. In one letter, she wrote: "You are unbearable and burdensome, and very hard to live with; all your good qualities are overshadowed by your conceit, and made useless to the world simply because you cannot restrain your propensity to pick holes in other people."[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-19) His mother, Johanna, was generally described as vivacious and sociable.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 9  She died 24 years later. Some of Arthur's negative opinions about women may be rooted in his troubled relationship with his mother.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-20)

Arthur moved to Hamburg to live with his friend Jean Anthime, who was also studying to become a merchant.

## Education

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Education")]

He moved to Weimar but did not live with his mother, who even tried to discourage him from coming by explaining that they would not get along very well.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 131  Their relationship deteriorated even further due to their temperamental differences. He accused his mother of being financially irresponsible, flirtatious and seeking to remarry, which he considered an insult to his father's memory.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 116,131  His mother, while professing her love to him, criticized him sharply for being moody, tactless, and argumentative, and urged him to improve his behavior so that he would not alienate people.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 129  Arthur concentrated on his studies, which were now going very well, and he also enjoyed the usual social life such as balls, parties and theater. By that time Johanna's famous salon was well established among local intellectuals and dignitaries, the most celebrated of them being [Goethe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_von_Goethe "Johann Wolfgang von Goethe"). Arthur attended her parties, usually when he knew that Goethe would be there—although the famous writer and statesman seemed not even to notice the young and unknown student. It is possible that Goethe kept a distance because Johanna warned him about her son's depressive and combative nature, or because Goethe was then on bad terms with Arthur's language instructor and roommate, [Franz Passow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Passow "Franz Passow").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 134  Schopenhauer was also captivated by the beautiful [Karoline Jagemann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karoline_Jagemann "Karoline Jagemann"), mistress of [Karl August, Grand Duke of Saxe-Weimar-Eisenach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_August,_Grand_Duke_of_Saxe-Weimar-Eisenach "Karl August, Grand Duke of Saxe-Weimar-Eisenach"), and he wrote to her his only known love poem.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 135  Despite his later celebration of asceticism and negative views of sexuality, Schopenhauer occasionally had sexual affairs—usually with women of lower social status, such as servants, actresses, and sometimes even paid prostitutes.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 21  In a letter to his friend Anthime he claims that such affairs continued even in his mature age and admits that he had two out-of-wedlock daughters (born in 1819 and 1836), both of whom died in infancy.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 25  In their youthful correspondence Arthur and Anthime were somewhat boastful and competitive about their sexual exploits—but Schopenhauer seemed aware that women usually did not find him very charming or physically attractive, and his desires often remained unfulfilled.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 22 

He left Weimar to become a student at the [University of Göttingen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_August_University_of_G%C3%B6ttingen "Georg August University of Göttingen") in 1809. There are no written reasons about why Schopenhauer chose that university instead of the then more famous [University of Jena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Jena "University of Jena"), but Göttingen was known as more modern and scientifically oriented, with less attention given to theology.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 140  Law or medicine were usual choices for young men of Schopenhauer's status who also needed career and income; he chose medicine due to his [scientific interests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_science "Natural science"). Among his notable professors were [Bernhard Friedrich Thibaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernhard_Friedrich_Thibaut "Bernhard Friedrich Thibaut"), [Arnold Hermann Ludwig Heeren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Hermann_Ludwig_Heeren "Arnold Hermann Ludwig Heeren"), [Johann Friedrich Blumenbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Friedrich_Blumenbach "Johann Friedrich Blumenbach"), [Friedrich Stromeyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Stromeyer "Friedrich Stromeyer"), [Heinrich Adolf Schrader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_Adolf_Schrader "Heinrich Adolf Schrader"), [Johann Tobias Mayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Tobias_Mayer "Johann Tobias Mayer") and [Konrad Johann Martin Langenbeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_Johann_Martin_Langenbeck "Konrad Johann Martin Langenbeck").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 141–144  He studied [metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics "Metaphysics"), [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology "Psychology") and [logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic "Logic") under [Gottlob Ernst Schulze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottlob_Ernst_Schulze "Gottlob Ernst Schulze"), the author of *[Aenesidemus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aenesidemus_\(book\) "Aenesidemus (book)")*, who made a strong impression and advised him to concentrate on [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato") and [Immanuel Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant "Immanuel Kant").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 144  He decided to switch from medicine to philosophy around 1810–11 and he left Göttingen, which did not have a strong philosophy program: besides Schulze, the only other philosophy professor was [Friedrich Bouterwek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Bouterwek "Friedrich Bouterwek"), whom Schopenhauer disliked.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 150  He did not regret his medicinal and scientific studies; he claimed that they were necessary for a philosopher, and even in Berlin he attended more lectures in sciences than in philosophy.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 170  During his days at Göttingen, he spent considerable time studying, but also continued his flute playing and social life. His friends included [Friedrich Gotthilf Osann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Gotthilf_Osann "Friedrich Gotthilf Osann"), [Karl Witte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Witte "Karl Witte"), [Christian Charles Josias von Bunsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Charles_Josias_von_Bunsen "Christian Charles Josias von Bunsen"), and [William Backhouse Astor Sr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Backhouse_Astor_Sr. "William Backhouse Astor Sr.")[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 151 

He arrived at the newly founded [University of Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Berlin "University of Berlin") for the winter semester of 1811–12. At the same time, his mother had just begun her literary career; she published her first book in 1810, a biography of her friend [Karl Ludwig Fernow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Ludwig_Fernow "Karl Ludwig Fernow"), which was a critical success. Arthur attended lectures by the prominent [post-Kantian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Kantian "Post-Kantian") philosopher [Johann Gottlieb Fichte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Gottlieb_Fichte "Johann Gottlieb Fichte"), but quickly found many points of disagreement with his *Wissenschaftslehre*; he also found Fichte's lectures tedious and hard to understand.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 159  He later mentioned Fichte only in critical, negative terms[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 159 —seeing his philosophy as a lower-quality version of Kant's and considering it useful only because Fichte's poor arguments unintentionally highlighted some failings of Kantianism.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 165–169  He also attended the lectures of the famous Protestant theologian [Friedrich Schleiermacher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Schleiermacher "Friedrich Schleiermacher"), whom he also quickly came to dislike.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 174  His notes and comments on Schleiermacher's lectures show that Schopenhauer was becoming very [critical of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_religion "Criticism of religion") and moving towards [atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism "Atheism").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 175  He learned by self-directed reading; besides Plato, Kant and Fichte he also read the works of [Schelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Wilhelm_Joseph_Schelling "Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling"), [Fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakob_Friedrich_Fries "Jakob Friedrich Fries"), [Jacobi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Heinrich_Jacobi "Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi"), [Bacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Bacon "Francis Bacon"), [Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke "John Locke"), and much current scientific literature.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 170  He attended philological courses by [August Böckh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_B%C3%B6ckh "August Böckh") and [Friedrich August Wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_August_Wolf "Friedrich August Wolf") and continued his naturalistic interests with courses by [Martin Heinrich Klaproth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Heinrich_Klaproth "Martin Heinrich Klaproth"), [Paul Erman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Erman "Paul Erman"), [Johann Elert Bode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Elert_Bode "Johann Elert Bode"), [Ernst Gottfried Fischer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Gottfried_Fischer "Ernst Gottfried Fischer"), [Johann Horkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Horkel "Johann Horkel"), [Friedrich Christian Rosenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Christian_Rosenthal "Friedrich Christian Rosenthal") and [Hinrich Lichtenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinrich_Lichtenstein "Hinrich Lichtenstein") (Lichtenstein was also a friend whom he met at one of his mother's parties in Weimar).[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 171–174 

## Early work

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Early work")]

Schopenhauer left Berlin in a rush in 1813, fearing that the city could be attacked and that he could be pressed into military service as Prussia had just joined the [war against France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Sixth_Coalition "War of the Sixth Coalition").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 179  He returned to Weimar but left after less than a month, disgusted by the fact that his mother was now living with her supposed lover, [Georg Friedrich Konrad Ludwig Müller von Gerstenbergk](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Georg_Friedrich_Konrad_Ludwig_M%C3%BCller_von_Gerstenbergk\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Georg Friedrich Konrad Ludwig Müller von Gerstenbergk (page does not exist)") \[[de](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Friedrich_von_Gerstenbergk "de:Georg Friedrich von Gerstenbergk")], a civil servant twelve years younger than her; he considered the relationship an act of infidelity to his father's memory.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 188  He settled for a while in [Rudolstadt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolstadt "Rudolstadt"), hoping that no army would pass through the small town. He spent his time in solitude, hiking in the mountains and the [Thuringian Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thuringian_Forest "Thuringian Forest") and writing his dissertation, *[On the Fourfold Root of the Principle of Sufficient Reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Fourfold_Root_of_the_Principle_of_Sufficient_Reason "On the Fourfold Root of the Principle of Sufficient Reason")*.

Schopenhauer completed his dissertation at about the same time as the French army was defeated at the [Battle of Leipzig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Leipzig "Battle of Leipzig"). He became irritated by the arrival of soldiers in the town and accepted his mother's invitation to visit her in Weimar. She tried to convince him that her relationship with Gerstenbergk was platonic and that she had no intention of remarrying.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 230  But Schopenhauer remained suspicious and often came in conflict with Gerstenbergk because he considered him untalented, pretentious, and [nationalistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_nationalism "German nationalism").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 231  His mother had just published her second book, *Reminiscences of a Journey in the Years 1803, 1804, and 1805*, a description of their family tour of Europe, which quickly became a hit. She found his dissertation incomprehensible and said it was unlikely that anyone would ever buy a copy. In a fit of temper Arthur told her that people would read his work long after the "rubbish" she wrote was totally forgotten.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-21)[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-22) In fact, although they considered her novels of dubious quality, the [Brockhaus publishing firm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F.A._Brockhaus_AG "F.A. Brockhaus AG") held her in high esteem because they consistently sold well. Hans Brockhaus later claimed that his predecessors "saw nothing in this manuscript, but wanted to please one of our best-selling authors by publishing her son's work. We published more and more of her son Arthur's work and today nobody remembers Johanna, but her son's works are in steady demand and contribute to Brockhaus' reputation."[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-mom-23) He kept large portraits of the pair in his office in [Leipzig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leipzig "Leipzig") for the edification of his new editors.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-mom-23)

Also contrary to his mother's prediction, Schopenhauer's dissertation made an impression on Goethe, to whom he sent it as a gift.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 241  Although it is doubtful that Goethe agreed with Schopenhauer's philosophical positions, he was impressed by his intellect and extensive scientific education.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 243  Their subsequent meetings and correspondence were a great honor to a young philosopher, who was finally acknowledged by his intellectual hero. They mostly discussed Goethe's newly published (and somewhat lukewarmly received) work on [color theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_Colours "Theory of Colours"). Schopenhauer soon started writing his own treatise on the subject, *[On Vision and Colors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_Vision_and_Colors "On Vision and Colors")*, which in many points differed from his teacher's. Although they remained polite towards each other, their growing theoretical disagreements—and especially Schopenhauer's extreme self-confidence and tactless criticisms—soon made Goethe become distant again and after 1816 their correspondence became less frequent.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 247–265  Schopenhauer later admitted that he was greatly hurt by this rejection, but he continued to praise Goethe, and considered his color theory a great introduction to his own.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 252,256,265 

Another important experience during his stay in Weimar was his acquaintance with Friedrich Majer[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24)—a [historian of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historian_of_religion "Historian of religion"), [orientalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_studies "Oriental studies") and disciple of [Herder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Gottfried_Herder "Johann Gottfried Herder")—who introduced him to [Eastern philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_philosophy "Eastern philosophy")[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199767%E2%80%9368-25)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 266  (see also [Indology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#Indology)). Schopenhauer was immediately impressed by the *[Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads "Upanishads")* (he called them "the production of the highest human wisdom", and believed that they contained superhuman concepts) and the [Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha "Buddha"),[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199767%E2%80%9368-25) and put them on a par with Plato and Kant.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 268,272  He continued his studies by reading the *[Bhagavad Gita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagavad_Gita "Bhagavad Gita")*, an amateurish German journal *Asiatisches Magazin*, and *Asiatick Researches* by [the Asiatic Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Asiatic_Society "The Asiatic Society").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 267,272  Schopenhauer held a profound respect for [Indian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_philosophy "Indian philosophy");[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199767%E2%80%9369-26) and loved [Hindu texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_texts "Hindu texts"). Although he never revered a Buddhist text he regarded [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") as the most distinguished religion.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke1997273-27)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 272  His studies on Hindu and Buddhist texts were constrained by the lack of adequate literature,[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199769-28) and the latter were mostly restricted to [Theravada Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada_Buddhism "Theravada Buddhism"). He also claimed that he formulated most of his ideas independently,[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199767%E2%80%9368-25) and only later realized the similarities with Buddhism.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 274–276 

Schopenhauer read the Latin translation and praised the Upanishads in his main work, *[The World as Will and Representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_as_Will_and_Representation "The World as Will and Representation")* (1819), as well as in his *[Parerga and Paralipomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parerga_and_Paralipomena "Parerga and Paralipomena")* (1851), and commented

> In the whole world there is no study so beneficial and so elevating as that of the Upanishads. It has been the solace of my life, it will be the solace of my death.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/Arthur_Schopenhauer_Portrait_by_Ludwig_Sigismund_Ruhl_1815.jpeg/220px-Arthur_Schopenhauer_Portrait_by_Ludwig_Sigismund_Ruhl_1815.jpeg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arthur_Schopenhauer_Portrait_by_Ludwig_Sigismund_Ruhl_1815.jpeg)

Schopenhauer in 1815. Portrait by Ludwig Sigismund Ruhl.

As the relationship with his mother fell to a new low, in May 1814 he left Weimar and moved to [Dresden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dresden "Dresden").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 265  He continued his philosophical studies, enjoyed the cultural life, socialized with intellectuals and engaged in sexual affairs.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 284  His friends in Dresden were [Johann Gottlob von Quandt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Gottlob_von_Quandt "Johann Gottlob von Quandt"), [Friedrich Laun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Laun "Friedrich Laun"), [Karl Christian Friedrich Krause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Christian_Friedrich_Krause "Karl Christian Friedrich Krause") and Ludwig Sigismund Ruhl, a young painter who made a romanticized portrait of him in which he improved some of Schopenhauer's unattractive physical features.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 278,283  His criticisms of local artists occasionally caused public quarrels when he ran into them in public.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 282  Schopenhauer's main occupation during his stay in Dresden was his seminal philosophical work, *[The World as Will and Representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_as_Will_and_Representation "The World as Will and Representation")*, which he started writing in 1814 and finished in 1818.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30) He was recommended to the publisher [Friedrich Arnold Brockhaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Arnold_Brockhaus "Friedrich Arnold Brockhaus") by Baron Ferdinand von Biedenfeld, an acquaintance of his mother.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 285  Although Brockhaus accepted his manuscript, Schopenhauer made a poor impression because of his quarrelsome and fussy attitude, as well as very poor sales of the book after it was published in December 1818.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 285–289 

In September 1818, while waiting for his book to be published and conveniently escaping an affair with a maid that caused an unwanted pregnancy,[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 342  Schopenhauer left Dresden for a year-long vacation in Italy.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 346  He visited [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice "Venice"), [Bologna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bologna "Bologna"), [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence "Florence"), [Naples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naples "Naples") and [Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milan "Milan"), travelling alone or accompanied by mostly English tourists he met.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 350  He spent the winter months in [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome "Rome"), where he accidentally met his acquaintance [Karl Witte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Witte "Karl Witte") and engaged in numerous quarrels with German tourists in the [Caffè Greco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antico_Caff%C3%A8_Greco "Antico Caffè Greco"), among them [Johann Friedrich Böhmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Friedrich_B%C3%B6hmer "Johann Friedrich Böhmer"), who also mentioned his insulting remarks and unpleasant character.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 348–349  He enjoyed art, architecture, and ancient ruins, attended plays and operas, and continued his philosophical contemplation and love affairs.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 346–350  One of his affairs supposedly became serious, and for a while he contemplated marriage to a rich Italian noblewoman—but, despite his mentioning this several times, no details are known and it may have been Schopenhauer exaggerating.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 345  He corresponded regularly with his sister Adele and became close to her as her relationship with Johanna and Gerstenbergk also deteriorated.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 344  She informed him about their financial troubles as the banking house of A. L. Muhl in Danzig—in which her mother invested their whole savings and Arthur a third of his—was near bankruptcy.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 351  Arthur offered to share his assets, but his mother refused and became further enraged by his insulting comments.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 352  The women managed to receive only thirty percent of their savings while Arthur, using his business knowledge, took a suspicious and aggressive stance towards the banker and eventually received his part in full.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 354–356  The affair additionally worsened the relationships among all three members of the Schopenhauer family.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 352,354 

He shortened his stay in Italy because of the trouble with Muhl and returned to Dresden.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 356  Disturbed by the financial risk and the lack of responses to his book he decided to take an academic position since it provided him with both income and an opportunity to promote his views.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 358  He contacted his friends at universities in Heidelberg, Göttingen and Berlin and found [Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humboldt_University_of_Berlin "Humboldt University of Berlin") most attractive.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 358–362  He scheduled his lectures to coincide with those of the famous philosopher [G. W. F. Hegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Friedrich_Hegel "Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel"), whom Schopenhauer described as a "clumsy charlatan".[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32) He was especially appalled by Hegel's supposedly poor knowledge of natural sciences and tried to engage him in a quarrel about it already at his test lecture in March 1820.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 363  Hegel was also facing political suspicions at the time, when many progressive professors were [fired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlsbad_Decrees "Carlsbad Decrees"), while Schopenhauer carefully mentioned in his application that he had no interest in politics.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 362  Despite their differences and the arrogant request to schedule lectures at the same time as his own, Hegel still voted to accept Schopenhauer to the university.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 365  Only five students turned up to Schopenhauer's lectures, and he dropped out of [academia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia "Academia"). A late essay, "On University Philosophy", expressed his resentment towards the work conducted in academies.

## Later life

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Later life")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Arthur_Shopengauer_by_Gennadij_Jerszow.jpg/170px-Arthur_Shopengauer_by_Gennadij_Jerszow.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arthur_Shopengauer_by_Gennadij_Jerszow.jpg)

Sculpture of Arthur Schopenhauer by [Giennadij Jerszow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giennadij_Jerszow "Giennadij Jerszow")

After his trying in academia, he continued to travel extensively, visiting [Leipzig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leipzig "Leipzig"), [Nuremberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg "Nuremberg"), [Stuttgart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuttgart "Stuttgart"), [Schaffhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schaffhausen "Schaffhausen"), [Vevey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vevey "Vevey"), Milan and spending eight months in Florence.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 411  Before he left for his three-year travel, Schopenhauer had an incident with his Berlin neighbor, 47-year-old seamstress Caroline Louise Marquet. The details of the August 1821 incident are unknown. He claimed that he had just pushed her from his entrance after she had rudely refused to leave, and that she had purposely fallen to the ground so that she could sue him. She claimed that he had attacked her so violently that she had become paralyzed on her right side and unable to work. She immediately sued him, and the process lasted until May 1827, when a court found Schopenhauer guilty and forced him to pay her an annual pension until her death in 1842.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 408–411 

Schopenhauer enjoyed Italy, where he studied art and socialized with Italian and English nobles.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 411–414  It was his last visit to the country. He left for [Munich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munich "Munich") and stayed there for a year, mostly recuperating from various health issues, some of them possibly caused by venereal diseases (the treatment his doctor used suggests [syphilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syphilis "Syphilis")).[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 415  He contacted publishers, offering to translate Hume into German and Kant into English, but his proposals were declined.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 417,422  Returning to Berlin, he began to study Spanish so he could read some of his favorite authors in their original language. He liked [Pedro Calderón de la Barca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Calder%C3%B3n_de_la_Barca "Pedro Calderón de la Barca"), [Lope de Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lope_de_Vega "Lope de Vega"), [Miguel de Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_de_Cervantes "Miguel de Cervantes"), and especially [Baltasar Gracián](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltasar_Graci%C3%A1n "Baltasar Gracián").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 420  He also made failed attempts to publish his translations of their works. A few attempts to revive his lectures—again scheduled at the same time as Hegel's—also failed, as did his inquiries about relocating to other universities.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 429–432 

During his Berlin years, Schopenhauer occasionally mentioned his desire to marry and have a family.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 404,432  For a while he was unsuccessfully courting 17-year-old Flora Weiss, who was 22 years younger than himself.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 433  His unpublished writings from that time show that he was already very critical of [monogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monogamy "Monogamy") but still not advocating [polygyny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygyny "Polygyny")—instead musing about a [polyamorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyamory "Polyamory") relationship that he called "tetragamy".[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 404–408  He had an on-and-off relationship with a young dancer, [Caroline Richter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Medon "Caroline Medon") (she also used the surname Medon after one of her ex-lovers).[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 403  They met when he was 33 and she was 19 and working at the Berlin Opera. She had already had numerous lovers and a son out of wedlock, and later gave birth to another son, this time to an unnamed foreign diplomat (she soon had another pregnancy but the child was stillborn).[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 403–404  As Schopenhauer was preparing to escape from Berlin in 1831, due to a [cholera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cholera "Cholera") epidemic, he offered to take her with him on the condition that she left her young son behind.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 404  She refused and he went alone; in his will he left her a significant sum of money, but insisted that it should not be spent in any way on her second son.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 404 

Schopenhauer claimed that, in his last year in Berlin, he had a [prophetic dream](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/premonition "wikt:premonition") that urged him to escape from the city.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 436  As he arrived in his new home in [Frankfurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurt "Frankfurt"), he supposedly had another [supernatural experience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernatural "Supernatural"), an apparition of his dead father and his mother, who was still alive.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 436  This experience led him to spend some time investigating [paranormal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paranormal "Paranormal") phenomena and [magic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_\(supernatural\) "Magic (supernatural)"). He was quite critical of the available studies and claimed that they were mostly ignorant or fraudulent, but he did believe that there are authentic cases of such phenomena and tried to explain them through his metaphysics as manifestations of the will.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 437–452 

Upon his arrival in Frankfurt, he experienced a period of depression and declining health.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 454  He renewed his correspondence with his mother, and she seemed concerned that he might commit suicide like his father.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 454–457  By now Johanna and Adele were living very modestly. Johanna's writing did not bring her much income, and her popularity was waning.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 458  Their correspondence remained reserved, and Arthur seemed undisturbed by her death in 1838.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 460  His relationship with his sister grew closer and he corresponded with her until she died in 1849.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 463 

In July 1832, Schopenhauer left Frankfurt for [Mannheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mannheim "Mannheim") but returned in July 1833 to remain there for the rest of his life, except for a few short journeys.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 464  He lived alone except for a succession of pet [poodles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poodle "Poodle") named [Atman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%80tman_\(Hinduism\) "Ātman (Hinduism)") and Butz. In 1836, he published *On the Will in Nature*. In 1838, he sent his essay "[On the Freedom of the Will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Freedom_of_the_Will "On the Freedom of the Will")" to the contest of the [Royal Norwegian Society of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Norwegian_Society_of_Sciences_and_Letters "Royal Norwegian Society of Sciences and Letters") in 1838 and won the prize in 1839. He sent another essay, "[On the Basis of Morality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Basis_of_Morality "On the Basis of Morality")", to the [Royal Danish Society of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Danish_Academy_of_Sciences_and_Letters "Royal Danish Academy of Sciences and Letters") in 1839, but did not win the (1840) prize despite being the only contestant.[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-33) The Society was appalled that several distinguished contemporary philosophers were mentioned in a very offensive manner, and claimed that the essay missed the point of the set topic and that the arguments were inadequate.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 483  Schopenhauer, who had been very confident that he would win, was enraged by this rejection. He published both essays as *The Two Basic Problems of Ethics*. The first edition, published September 1840 but with an 1841 date, again failed to draw attention to his philosophy. In the preface to the second edition, in 1860, he was still pouring insults on the Royal Danish Society.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 484  Two years later, after some negotiations, he managed to convince his publisher, Brockhaus, to print the second, updated edition of *The World as Will and Representation*. That book was again mostly ignored and the few reviews were mixed or negative.

Schopenhauer began to attract some followers, mostly outside academia, among practical professionals (several of them were lawyers) who pursued private philosophical studies. He jokingly referred to them as "evangelists" and "apostles".[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 504  One of the most active early followers was [Julius Frauenstädt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Frauenst%C3%A4dt "Julius Frauenstädt"), who wrote numerous articles promoting Schopenhauer's philosophy. He was also instrumental in finding another publisher after Brockhaus declined to publish *Parerga and Paralipomena*, believing that it would be another failure.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 506  Though Schopenhauer later stopped corresponding with him, claiming that he did not adhere closely enough to his ideas, Frauenstädt continued to promote Schopenhauer's work.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 507–508  They renewed their communication in 1859 and Schopenhauer named him heir for his literary estate.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 508  Frauenstädt also became the editor of the first collected works of Schopenhauer.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 506 

In 1848, Schopenhauer witnessed [violent upheaval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_revolutions_of_1848%E2%80%9349 "German revolutions of 1848–49") in Frankfurt after General [Hans Adolf Erdmann von Auerswald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Adolf_Erdmann_von_Auerswald "Hans Adolf Erdmann von Auerswald") and Prince [Felix Lichnowsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felix_Lichnowsky "Felix Lichnowsky") were murdered. He became worried for his own safety and property.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 514  Even earlier in life he had had such worries and kept a sword and loaded pistols near his bed to defend himself from thieves.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 465  He gave a friendly welcome to Austrian soldiers who wanted to shoot revolutionaries from his window and as they were leaving he gave one of the officers his opera glasses to help him monitor rebels.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 514  The rebellion passed without any loss to Schopenhauer and he later praised [Alfred I, Prince of Windisch-Grätz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_I,_Prince_of_Windisch-Gr%C3%A4tz "Alfred I, Prince of Windisch-Grätz") for restoring order.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 515  He even modified his will, leaving a large part of his property to a Prussian fund that helped soldiers who became invalids while fighting rebellion in 1848 or the families of soldiers who died in battle.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 517  As [Young Hegelians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Hegelians "Young Hegelians") were advocating change and progress, Schopenhauer claimed that misery is natural for humans and that, even if some utopian society were established, people would still fight each other out of boredom, or would starve due to overpopulation.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 515 

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Schopenhauer.jpg/220px-Schopenhauer.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Schopenhauer.jpg)

1855 painting of Schopenhauer by [Jules Lunteschütz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Luntesch%C3%BCtz "Jules Lunteschütz")

In 1851, Schopenhauer published *[Parerga and Paralipomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parerga_and_Paralipomena "Parerga and Paralipomena")*, which contains essays that are supplementary to his main work. It was his first successful, widely read book, partly due to the work of his disciples who wrote praising reviews.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 524  The essays that proved most popular were the ones that actually did not contain the basic philosophical ideas of his system.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 539  Many academic philosophers considered him a great stylist and cultural critic but did not take his philosophy seriously.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 539  His early critics liked to point out similarities of his ideas to those of Fichte and Schelling,[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 381–386  or to claim that there were numerous contradictions in his philosophy.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 381–386, 537  Both criticisms enraged Schopenhauer. He was becoming less interested in intellectual fights, but encouraged his disciples to do so.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 525  His private notes and correspondence show that he acknowledged some of the criticisms regarding contradictions, inconsistencies, and vagueness in his philosophy, but claimed that he was not concerned about harmony and agreement in his propositions[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 394  and that some of his ideas should not be taken literally but instead as metaphors.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 510 

Academic philosophers were also starting to notice his work. In 1856, the University of Leipzig sponsored an essay contest about Schopenhauer's philosophy, which was won by [Rudolf Seydel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Seydel "Rudolf Seydel")'s very critical essay.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 536  Schopenhauer's friend [Jules Lunteschütz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Luntesch%C3%BCtz "Jules Lunteschütz") made the first of his four portraits of him—which Schopenhauer did not particularly like—which was soon sold to a wealthy landowner, Carl Ferdinand Wiesike, who built a house to display it. Schopenhauer seemed flattered and amused by this, and would claim that it was his first chapel.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 540  As his fame increased, copies of paintings and photographs of him were being sold and admirers were visiting the places where he had lived and written his works. People visited Frankfurt's *Englischer Hof* to observe him dining. Admirers gave him gifts and asked for autographs.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 541  He complained that he still felt isolated due to his not very social nature and the fact that many of his good friends had already died from old age.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 542 

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Schopenhauer-ffm001.jpg/220px-Schopenhauer-ffm001.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Schopenhauer-ffm001.jpg)

Grave at the *[Hauptfriedhof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurt_Main_Cemetery "Frankfurt Main Cemetery")* in [Frankfurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurt "Frankfurt")

He remained healthy in his own old age, which he attributed to regular walks no matter the weather and always getting enough sleep.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 544–545  He had a great appetite and could read without glasses, but his [hearing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hearing_loss "Hearing loss") had been declining since his youth and he developed problems with [rheumatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rheumatism "Rheumatism").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 545  He remained active and lucid, continued his reading, writing and correspondence until his death.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 545  The numerous notes that he made during these years, amongst others on aging, were published [posthumously](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posthumous_publication "Posthumous publication") under the title *Senilia*. In the spring of 1860 his health began to decline, and he experienced shortness of breath and heart palpitations; in September he suffered inflammation of the lungs and, although he was starting to recover, he remained very weak.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 546  The last friend to visit him was Wilhelm Gwinner; according to him, Schopenhauer was concerned that he would not be able to finish his planned additions to *Parerga and Paralipomena* but was at peace with dying.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 546–547  He died of [pulmonary-respiratory failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_failure "Respiratory failure")[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-34) on 21 September 1860 while sitting at home on his couch. He died at the age of 72 and had a funeral conducted by a Lutheran minister.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-35)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-36)

## Philosophy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Philosophy")]

### Theory of perception

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Theory of perception")]

In November 1813 [Goethe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_von_Goethe "Johann Wolfgang von Goethe") invited Schopenhauer to help him on his [Theory of Colours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_Colours "Theory of Colours"). Although Schopenhauer considered colour theory a minor matter,[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-37) he accepted the invitation out of admiration for Goethe. Nevertheless, these investigations led him to his most important discovery in epistemology: finding a demonstration for the *a priori* nature of causality.

Kant openly admitted that it was [Hume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hume "David Hume")'s skeptical assault on causality that motivated the critical investigations in *[Critique of Pure Reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critique_of_Pure_Reason "Critique of Pure Reason")* and gave an elaborate proof to show that causality is *a priori*. After [G. E. Schulze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottlob_Ernst_Schulze "Gottlob Ernst Schulze") had made it plausible that Kant had not disproven Hume's skepticism, it was up to those loyal to Kant's project to prove this important matter.

The difference between the approaches of Kant and Schopenhauer was this: Kant simply declared that the empirical content of perception is "given" to us from outside, an expression with which Schopenhauer often expressed his dissatisfaction.[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38) He, on the other hand, was occupied with the questions: how do we get this empirical content of perception; how is it possible to comprehend subjective sensations "limited to my skin" as the objective perception of things that lie "outside" of me?[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-39)

> The sensations in the hand of a man born blind, on feeling an object of cubic shape, are quite uniform and the same on all sides and in every direction: the edges, it is true, press upon a smaller portion of his hand, still nothing at all like a cube is contained in these sensations. His Understanding draws the immediate and intuitive conclusion from the resistance felt, that this resistance must have a cause, which then presents itself through that conclusion as a hard body; and through the movements of his arms in feeling the object, while the hand's sensation remains unaltered, he constructs the cubic shape in Space. If the representation of a cause and of Space, together with their laws, had not already existed within him, the image of a cube could never have proceeded from those successive sensations in his hand.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-40)

Causality is therefore not an empirical concept drawn from objective perceptions, as Hume had maintained; instead, as Kant had said, objective perception presupposes knowledge of causality.[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-41)

By this intellectual operation, comprehending every effect in our sensory organs as having an external cause, the external world arises. With vision, finding the cause is essentially simplified due to light acting in straight lines. We are seldom conscious of the process that interprets the double sensation in both eyes as coming from one object, that inverts the impressions on the retinas, and that uses the change in the apparent position of an object relative to more distant objects provided by binocular vision to perceive depth and distance.

Schopenhauer stresses the importance of the intellectual nature of perception; the senses furnish the raw material by which the intellect produces the world as representation. He set out his theory of perception for the first time in *[On Vision and Colors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_Vision_and_Colors "On Vision and Colors")*,[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-42) and, in the subsequent editions of *Fourfold Root*, an extensive exposition is given in § 21.

### World as representation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: World as representation")]

Schopenhauer saw his philosophy as an extension of Kant's, and used the results of Kant's theoretical and epistemological investigations ([transcendental idealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcendental_idealism "Transcendental idealism")) as starting point for his own. Kant had argued that the [empirical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_evidence "Empirical evidence") world is merely a complex of appearances whose existence and connection occur only in our [mental representations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_representation "Mental representation").[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-43) Schopenhauer did not deny that the external world existed and was known empirically, yet he followed Kant in claiming that our knowledge and experience of the world is always in some sense dependent on *us*.[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-44) For Schopenhauer in particular, the spatiotemporal form and causal structure of the external world are contributed to our experiences of it by the mind as it renders perceptions.[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-On_the_Fourfold_Root_of_the_Principle_of_Sufficient_Reason,_and_On_the_Will_in_Nature:_Two_Essays_\(revised_edition\)-45) Schopenhauer reiterates this in the first sentence of his main work: "The world is my representation (*Die Welt ist meine Vorstellung*)". Everything that there is for cognition (the entire world) exists simply as an object in relation to a subject—a 'representation' to a subject. Everything that belongs to the world is, therefore, 'subject-dependent'. In Book One of *The World as Will and Representation*, Schopenhauer considers the world from this angle—that is, insofar as it is representation.

Kant had previously argued that we perceive reality as something spatial and temporal not because reality is inherently spatial and temporal, but because that is how our minds operate in perceiving an object. Therefore, understanding objects in space and time represents our 'contribution' to an experience. For Schopenhauer, Kant's 'greatest service' lay in the 'differentiation between [phenomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenomena "Phenomena") and the thing-in-itself ([noumena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noumena "Noumena")), based on the proof that between everything and us there is always a perceiving mind.' In other words, Kant's primary achievement is to demonstrate that instead of being a blank slate where reality merely reveals its character, the mind, with sensory support, actively participates in constructing reality. Thus, Schopenhauer believed that Kant had shown that the everyday world of experience, and indeed the entire material world related to space and time, is merely 'appearance' or 'phenomena,' entirely distinct from the thing-in-itself.'[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46)

### World as will

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: World as will")]

Main article: [The World as Will and Representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_as_Will_and_Representation "The World as Will and Representation")

In Book Two of *The World as Will and Representation*, Schopenhauer considers what the world is beyond the aspect of it that appears to us—that is, the aspect of the world beyond representation, the world considered "[in-itself](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thing-in-itself "Thing-in-itself")" or "[noumena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noumena "Noumena")", its inner essence. The very being in-itself of all things, Schopenhauer argues, is will (*Wille*). The empirical world that appears to us as representation has plurality and is ordered in a spatio-temporal framework. The world as thing in-itself must exist outside the subjective forms of space and time. Although the world manifests itself to our experience as a multiplicity of objects (the "objectivation" of the will), each element of this multiplicity has the same blind essence striving towards existence and life. Human rationality is merely a secondary phenomenon that does not distinguish humanity from the rest of nature at the fundamental, essential level. The advanced cognitive abilities of human beings, Schopenhauer argues, serve the ends of willing—an illogical, directionless, ceaseless striving that condemns the human individual to a life of suffering unredeemed by any final purpose. Schopenhauer's philosophy of the will as the essential reality behind the world as representation is often called [metaphysical voluntarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voluntarism_\(philosophy\) "Voluntarism (philosophy)").[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Brit-3)

For Schopenhauer, understanding the world as will leads to ethical concerns (see the [ethics section below](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#Ethics) for further detail), which he explores in the Fourth Book of *The World as Will and Representation* and again in his two prize essays on ethics, *[On the Freedom of the Will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Freedom_of_the_Will "On the Freedom of the Will")* and *[On the Basis of Morality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Basis_of_Morality "On the Basis of Morality")*. No individual human actions are free, Schopenhauer argues, because they are events in the world of appearance and thus are subject to the principle of sufficient reason: a person's actions are a necessary consequence of motives and the given character of the individual human. Necessity extends to the actions of human beings just as it does to every other appearance, and thus we cannot speak of freedom of individual willing. Albert Einstein quoted the Schopenhauerian idea that "a man can *do* as he will, but not *will* as he will."[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-47) Yet the will as thing in-itself is free, as it exists beyond the realm of representation and thus is not constrained by any of the forms of necessity that are part of the principle of sufficient reason.

According to Schopenhauer, salvation from our miserable existence can come through the will's being "tranquillized" by the metaphysical insight that reveals individuality to be merely an illusion. The saint or 'great soul' intuitively "recognizes the whole, comprehends its essence, and finds that it is constantly passing away, caught up in vain strivings, inner conflict, and perpetual suffering".[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-48) The negation of the will, in other words, stems from the insight that the world in-itself (free from the forms of space and time) is one. [Ascetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asceticism "Asceticism") practices, Schopenhauer remarks, are used to aid the will's "self-abolition", which brings about a blissful, redemptive "will-less" state of emptiness that is free from striving or suffering.

### Art and aesthetics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Art and aesthetics")]

Main article: [Arthur Schopenhauer's aesthetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Schopenhauer%27s_aesthetics "Arthur Schopenhauer's aesthetics")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Johannes_Vermeer_-_Het_melkmeisje_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-Johannes_Vermeer_-_Het_melkmeisje_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Johannes_Vermeer_-_Het_melkmeisje_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg)

In his main work, Schopenhauer praised the [Dutch Golden Age artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Golden_Age_painting "Dutch Golden Age painting"), who "directed such purely objective perception to the most insignificant objects, and set up a lasting monument of their objectivity and spiritual peace in paintings of *[still life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Still_life "Still life")*. The aesthetic beholder does not contemplate this without emotion."[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-49)

For Schopenhauer, human "willing"—desiring, craving, etc.—is at the root of [suffering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffering "Suffering"). A temporary way to escape this pain is through aesthetic contemplation. Here one moves away from ordinary cognizance of individual things to cognizance of eternal Platonic *Ideas*—in other words, cognizance that is free from the service of will. In aesthetic contemplation, one no longer perceives an object of perception as something from which one is separated; rather "it is as if the object alone existed without anyone perceiving it, and one can thus no longer separate the perceiver from the perception, but the two have become one, the entirety of consciousness entirely filled and occupied by a single perceptual image".[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-50) Subject and object are no longer distinguishable, and the *Idea* comes to the fore.

From this aesthetic immersion, one is no longer an individual who suffers as a result of servitude to one's individual will but, rather, becomes a "pure, will-less, painless, timeless, subject of cognition". The pure, will-less subject of cognition is cognizant only of Ideas, not individual things: this is a kind of cognition that is unconcerned with relations between objects according to the Principle of Sufficient Reason (time, space, cause and effect) and instead involves complete absorption in the object.

Art is the practical consequence of this brief aesthetic contemplation, since it attempts to depict the essence/pure Ideas of the world. Music, for Schopenhauer, is the purest form of art because it is the one that depicts the will itself without it appearing as subject to the Principle of Sufficient Reason, therefore as an individual object. According to [Daniel Albright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Albright "Daniel Albright"), "Schopenhauer thought that [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_music "Philosophy of music") was the only art that did not merely copy ideas, but actually embodied the will itself".[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-51) He deemed music a timeless, universal language comprehended everywhere, that can imbue global enthusiasm, if in possession of a significant melody.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Music-52)

### Mathematics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Mathematics")]

Schopenhauer's [realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_realism "Mathematical realism") views on mathematics are evident in his criticism of contemporaneous attempts to prove the [parallel postulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_postulate "Parallel postulate") in [Euclidean geometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclidean_geometry "Euclidean geometry"). Writing shortly before the discovery of [hyperbolic geometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperbolic_geometry "Hyperbolic geometry") demonstrated the logical independence of the [axiom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axiom "Axiom")—and long before the [general theory of relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_theory_of_relativity "General theory of relativity") revealed that it does not necessarily express a property of physical space—Schopenhauer criticized mathematicians for trying to use indirect [concepts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concept "Concept") to prove what he held was directly evident from [intuitive perception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intuition "Intuition").

> The Euclidean method of demonstration has brought forth from its own womb its most striking parody and caricature in the famous controversy over the theory of *parallels*, and in the attempts, repeated every year, to prove the eleventh axiom (also known as the fifth postulate). The axiom asserts, and that indeed through the indirect criterion of a third intersecting line, that two lines inclined to each other (for this is the precise meaning of "less than two right angles"), if produced far enough, must meet. Now this truth is supposed to be too complicated to pass as self-evident, and therefore needs a proof; but no such proof can be produced, just because there is nothing more immediate.[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ReferenceB-53)

Throughout his writings,[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-54) Schopenhauer criticized the logical derivation of philosophies and mathematics from mere concepts, instead of from intuitive perceptions.

> In fact, it seems to me that the logical method is in this way reduced to an absurdity. But it is precisely through the controversies over this, together with the futile attempts to demonstrate the *directly* certain as merely *indirectly* certain, that the independence and clearness of intuitive evidence appear in contrast with the uselessness and difficulty of logical proof, a contrast as instructive as it is amusing. The direct certainty will not be admitted here, just because it is no merely logical certainty following from the concept, and thus resting solely on the relation of predicate to subject, according to the principle of contradiction. But that eleventh axiom regarding parallel lines is a [synthetic proposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_proposition "Synthetic proposition") *[a priori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_priori_and_a_posteriori "A priori and a posteriori")*, and as such has the guarantee of pure, not empirical, perception; this perception is just as immediate and certain as is the [principle of contradiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principle_of_contradiction "Principle of contradiction") itself, from which all proofs originally derive their certainty. At bottom this holds good of every geometrical theorem ...

Although Schopenhauer could see no justification for trying to prove Euclid's parallel postulate, he did see a reason for examining another of Euclid's axioms.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-55)

> It surprises me that the eighth axiom,[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-56) "Figures that coincide with one another are equal to one another", is not rather attacked. For *"coinciding with one another"* is either a mere [tautology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tautology_\(logic\) "Tautology (logic)"), or something quite [empirical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical "Empirical"), belonging not to pure intuition or perception, but to external sensuous experience. Thus it presupposes mobility of the figures, but [matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matter "Matter") alone is movable in [space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space "Space"). Consequently, this reference to coincidence with one another forsakes pure space, the sole element of [geometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geometry "Geometry"), in order to pass over to the material and empirical.[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ReferenceB-53)

This follows [Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant "Immanuel Kant")'s reasoning.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-57)

### Ethics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Ethics")]

Main article: [On the Basis of Morality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Basis_of_Morality "On the Basis of Morality")

Schopenhauer asserts that the task of ethics is not to prescribe moral actions that ought to be done, but to investigate moral actions. As such, he states that philosophy is always theoretical: its task to explain what is given.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58)

According to Kant's transcendental idealism, space and time are forms of our sensibility in which phenomena appear in multiplicity. Reality [in itself](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thing-in-itself "Thing-in-itself") is free from multiplicity, not in the sense that an object is one, but that it is outside the *possibility* of multiplicity. Two individuals, though they appear distinct, are in-themselves not distinct.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-59)

Appearances are entirely subordinated to the [principle of sufficient reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principle_of_sufficient_reason "Principle of sufficient reason"). The egoistic individual who focuses his aims on his own interests has to deal with empirical laws as well as he can.

What is relevant for ethics are individuals who can act against their own self-interest. If we take a man who suffers when he sees his fellow men living in poverty and consequently uses a significant part of his income to support *their* needs instead of his *own* pleasures, then the simplest way to describe this is that he makes *less distinction between himself* and others than is usually made.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60)

Regarding how things *appear* to us, the egoist asserts a gap between two individuals, but the altruist experiences the sufferings of others as his own. In the same way a compassionate man cannot hurt animals, though they appear as distinct from himself.

What motivates the altruist is compassion. The suffering of others is for him not a cold matter to which he is indifferent, but he feels connectiveness to all beings. Compassion is thus the basis of morality.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-61)

#### Eternal justice
