\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: Eternal justice")]

Schopenhauer calls the principle through which multiplicity appears the *[principium individuationis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principium_individuationis "Principium individuationis")*. When we behold nature we see that it is a cruel battle for existence. Individual manifestations of the will can maintain themselves only at the expense of others—the will, as the only thing that exists, has no other option but to devour itself to experience pleasure. This is a fundamental characteristic of the will, and cannot be circumvented.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-62)

Unlike temporal or human justice, which requires time to repay an evil deed and "has its seat in the state, as requiting and punishing",[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-World_as_will_and_idea_Vol._1_%C2%A7_63-63) eternal justice "rules not the state but the world, is not dependent upon human institutions, is not subject to chance and deception, is not uncertain, wavering, and erring, but infallible, fixed, and sure".[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-World_as_will_and_idea_Vol._1_%C2%A7_63-63) Eternal justice is not retributive, because retribution requires time. There are no delays or reprieves. Instead, punishment is tied to the offence, "to the point where the two become one. ... Tormenter and tormented are one. The \[Tormenter] errs in that he believes he is not a partaker in the suffering; the \[tormented], in that he believes he is not a partaker in the guilt."[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-World_as_will_and_idea_Vol._1_%C2%A7_63-63)

Suffering is the moral outcome of our attachment to pleasure. Schopenhauer deemed that this truth was expressed by the Christian dogma of [original sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_sin "Original sin") and, in Eastern religions, by the [dogma of rebirth.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reincarnation "Reincarnation")

#### Quietism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Quietism")]

He who sees through the *principium individuationis* and comprehends suffering *in general* as his own will see suffering everywhere and, instead of fighting for the happiness of his individual manifestation, will abhor life itself since he knows that it is inseparably connected with suffering. For him, a happy individual life in a world of suffering is like a beggar who dreams one night that he is a king.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Ascetic-64)

Those who have experienced this intuitive knowledge cannot affirm life, but exhibit asceticism and quietism, meaning that they are no longer sensitive to motives, are not concerned about their individual welfare, and accept without resistance the evil that others inflict on them. They welcome poverty and neither seek nor flee death.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Ascetic-64) Schopenhauer referred to asceticism as the denial of the will to live.

Human life is a ceaseless struggle for satisfaction and, instead of continuing their struggle, ascetics break it. It does not matter if these ascetics adhere to the dogmata of Christianity or to [Dharmic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharmic_faith "Dharmic faith"), since their way of living is the result of intuitive knowledge.

> The [Christian mystic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_mysticism "Christian mysticism") and the teacher of the [Vedanta philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedanta_philosophy "Vedanta philosophy") agree in this respect also, they both regard all outward works and religious exercises as superfluous for him who has attained to perfection. So much agreement in the case of such different ages and nations is a practical proof that what is expressed here is not, as optimistic dullness likes to assert, an eccentricity and perversity of the mind, but an essential side of human nature, which only appears so rarely because of its excellence.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Ascetic-64)

### Psychology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Psychology")]

Philosophers have not traditionally been impressed by the necessity of sex, but Schopenhauer addressed sex and related concepts forthrightly:

> ... one ought rather to be surprised that a thing \[sex] which plays throughout so important a part in human life has hitherto practically been disregarded by philosophers altogether, and lies before us as raw and untreated material.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-65)

He named a force within man that he felt took invariable precedence over reason: the Will to Live or Will to Life (*Wille zum Leben*), defined as an inherent drive within human beings, and all creatures, to stay alive; a force that inveigles[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-inveigles-66) us into reproducing.

Schopenhauer refused to conceive of love as either trifling or accidental, but rather understood it as an immensely powerful force that lay unseen within man's [psyche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psyche_\(psychology\) "Psyche (psychology)"), guaranteeing the quality of the human race:

> The ultimate aim of all love affairs ... is more important than all other aims in man's life; and therefore it is quite worthy of the profound seriousness with which everyone pursues it. What is decided by it is nothing less than the composition of the next generation ...[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-67)

It has often been argued that Schopenhauer's thoughts on sexuality foreshadowed the [theory of evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution "Evolution"), a claim met with satisfaction by [Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin "Charles Darwin") as he included a quotation from Schopenhauer in his *[Descent of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Descent_of_Man,_and_Selection_in_Relation_to_Sex "The Descent of Man, and Selection in Relation to Sex")*.[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-68) This has also been noted about [Freud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmund_Freud "Sigmund Freud")'s concepts of the [libido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libido "Libido") and the [unconscious mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unconscious_mind "Unconscious mind"), and [evolutionary psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology "Evolutionary psychology") in general.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-69)

### Political and social thought

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: Political and social thought")]

#### Politics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: Politics")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/FFM_Wallanlagen_Schopenhauer-Denkmal.jpg/220px-FFM_Wallanlagen_Schopenhauer-Denkmal.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:FFM_Wallanlagen_Schopenhauer-Denkmal.jpg)

Bust in [Frankfurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurt "Frankfurt")

Schopenhauer's politics were an echo of his system of ethics, which he elucidated in detail in his *Die beiden Grundprobleme der Ethik* (the two essays *On the Freedom of the Will* and *On the Basis of Morality*).

In occasional political comments in his *[Parerga and Paralipomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parerga_and_Paralipomena "Parerga and Paralipomena")* and *Manuscript Remains*, Schopenhauer described himself as a proponent of [limited government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited_government "Limited government"). Schopenhauer shared the view of [Thomas Hobbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hobbes "Thomas Hobbes") on the necessity of the state and state action to check the innate destructive tendencies of our species. He also defended the independence of the legislative, judicial and executive branches of power, and a monarch as an impartial element able to practise justice (in a practical and everyday sense, not a cosmological one).[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-70)

He declared that monarchy is "natural to man in almost the same way as it is to bees and ants, to cranes in flight, to wandering elephants, to wolves in a pack in search of prey, and to other animals".[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Paralipomena,_Vol_p._254-71) Intellect in monarchies, he writes, always has "much better chances against stupidity, its implacable and ever-present foe, than it has in republics; but this is a great advantage."[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Paralipomena,_Vol_p._254-71) On the other hand, Schopenhauer disparaged [republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism "Republicanism") as being "as unnatural to man as it is unfavorable to higher intellectual life and thus to the arts and sciences".[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72)

By his own admission, Schopenhauer did not give much thought to politics, and several times he wrote proudly of how little attention he paid "to political affairs of \[his] day". In a life that spanned several revolutions in French and German government, and a few continent-shaking wars, he maintained his position of "minding not the times but the eternities". He wrote many disparaging remarks about Germany and the Germans. A typical example is: "For a German it is even good to have somewhat lengthy words in his mouth, for he thinks slowly, and they give him time to reflect."[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-73)

#### Punishment

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Punishment")]

The State, Schopenhauer claimed, punishes criminals to prevent future crimes. It places "beside every possible motive for committing a wrong a more powerful motive for leaving it undone, in the inescapable punishment. Accordingly, the criminal code is as complete a register as possible of counter-motives to all criminal actions that can possibly be imagined ..."[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-twwr62-74) He claimed that this doctrine was not original to him but had appeared in the writings of [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato"),[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-75) [Seneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seneca_the_Younger "Seneca the Younger"), [Hobbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hobbes "Thomas Hobbes"), [Pufendorf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_von_Pufendorf "Samuel von Pufendorf"), and [Anselm Feuerbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Johann_Anselm_Ritter_von_Feuerbach "Paul Johann Anselm Ritter von Feuerbach").

#### Races and religions

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Races and religions")]

Schopenhauer attributed civilizational primacy to the northern "white races" due to their sensitivity and creativity (except for the ancient Egyptians and Hindus, whom he saw as equal):

> The highest civilization and culture, apart from the [ancient Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Hinduism "History of Hinduism") and [Egyptians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt "Ancient Egypt"), are found exclusively among the white races; and even with many dark peoples, the ruling caste or race is fairer in colour than the rest and has, therefore, evidently immigrated, for example, the [Brahmans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmans "Brahmans"), the [Incas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inca_Empire "Inca Empire"), and the rulers of the [South Sea Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sea_Islands "South Sea Islands"). All this is due to the fact that necessity is the mother of invention because those tribes that emigrated early to the north, and there gradually became white, had to develop all their intellectual powers and invent and perfect all the arts in their struggle with need, want and misery, which in their many forms were brought about by the climate. This they had to do in order to make up for the parsimony of nature and out of it all came their high civilization.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-76)

Schopenhauer was fervently [opposed to slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism "Abolitionism"). Speaking of the treatment of slaves in the [slave-holding states of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States "Slavery in the United States"), he condemned "those devils in human form, those bigoted, church-going, strict sabbath-observing scoundrels, especially the Anglican parsons among them" for how they "treat their innocent black brothers who through violence and injustice have fallen into their devil's claws". The slave-holding states of North America, Schopenhauer writes, are a "disgrace to the whole of humanity".[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77)

Schopenhauer also maintained a marked metaphysical and political [anti-Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Judaism "Anti-Judaism"). He argued that Christianity constituted a revolt against what he styled the materialistic basis of Judaism, exhibiting an Indian-influenced ethics reflecting the [Aryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryan "Aryan")-[Vedic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas "Vedas") theme of spiritual self-conquest. He saw this as opposed to the ignorant drive toward earthly utopianism and superficiality of a worldly "Jewish" spirit:

> \[Judaism] is, therefore, the crudest and poorest of all religions and consists merely in an absurd and revolting [theism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theism "Theism"). It amounts to this that the [*κύριος* \['Lord'\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrios "Kyrios"), who has created the world, desires to be worshipped and adored; and so above all he is jealous, is envious of his colleagues, of all the other gods; if sacrifices are made to them he is furious and his Jews have a bad time ... It is most deplorable that this religion has become the basis of the prevailing religion of Europe; for it is a religion without any metaphysical tendency. While all other religions endeavor to explain to the people by symbols the metaphysical significance of life, the religion of the Jews is entirely immanent and furnishes nothing but a mere war-cry in the struggle with other nations.[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78)

#### Women

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=19 "Edit section: Women")]

In his 1851 essay "On Women", Schopenhauer expressed opposition to what he called "Teutonico-Christian stupidity" of "reflexive, unexamined reverence for the female (*abgeschmackten Weiberveneration*)".[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-79) He wrote: "Women are directly fitted for acting as the nurses and teachers of our early childhood by the fact that they are themselves childish, frivolous and short-sighted." He opined that women are deficient in artistic faculties and sense of justice, and expressed his opposition to [monogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monogamy "Monogamy").[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-80) He claimed that "woman is by nature meant to obey". The essay does give some compliments: "women are decidedly more sober in their judgment than \[men] are", and are more sympathetic to the suffering of others.

Schopenhauer's writings influenced many, from [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche "Friedrich Nietzsche") to nineteenth-century [feminists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminists "Feminists").[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81) His [biological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology "Biology") analysis of the difference between the sexes, and their separate roles in the struggle for survival and reproduction, anticipates some of the claims that were later ventured by [sociobiologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociobiology "Sociobiology") and [evolutionary psychologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology "Evolutionary psychology").[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Young2005-82)

When the elderly Schopenhauer sat for [a sculpture portrait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Schopenhauer_\(sculpture\) "Arthur Schopenhauer (sculpture)") by the Prussian sculptor [Elisabet Ney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisabet_Ney "Elisabet Ney") in 1859, he was much impressed by the young woman's wit and independence, as well as by her skill as a visual artist.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-83) After his time with Ney, he told Richard Wagner's friend [Malwida von Meysenbug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malwida_von_Meysenbug "Malwida von Meysenbug"): "I have not yet spoken my last word about women. I believe that if a woman succeeds in withdrawing from the mass, or rather raising herself above the mass, she grows ceaselessly and more than a man."[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-84)

#### Pederasty

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=20 "Edit section: Pederasty")]

In the third, expanded edition of *The World as Will and Representation* (1859), Schopenhauer added an appendix to his chapter on the *Metaphysics of Sexual Love*. He wrote that [pederasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pederasty "Pederasty") has the benefit of preventing ill-begotten children. Concerning this, he stated that "the vice we are considering appears to work directly against the aims and ends of nature, and that in a matter that is all important and of the greatest concern to her it must in fact serve these very aims, although only indirectly, as a means for preventing greater evils."[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85) Schopenhauer ends the appendix with the statement that "by expounding these paradoxical ideas, I wanted to grant to the professors of philosophy a small favour. I have done so by giving them the opportunity of slandering me by saying that I defend and commend pederasty."[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86)

#### Heredity and eugenics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Heredity and eugenics")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Frankfurt_Am_Main-Portraits-Arthur_Schopenhauer-1845.jpg/170px-Frankfurt_Am_Main-Portraits-Arthur_Schopenhauer-1845.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Frankfurt_Am_Main-Portraits-Arthur_Schopenhauer-1845.jpg)

Schopenhauer at age 58 on 16 May 1846

Schopenhauer viewed personality and [intellect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intellect "Intellect") as inherited. He quotes [Horace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace "Horace")'s saying, "From the brave and good are the brave descended" (*Odes*, iv, 4, 29) and Shakespeare's line from *[Cymbeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cymbeline "Cymbeline")*, "Cowards father cowards, and base things sire base" (IV, 2) to reinforce his hereditarian argument.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-87) Mechanistically, Schopenhauer believed that a person inherits his intellect through his mother, and personal character through the father.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88) This belief in heritability of traits informed Schopenhauer's view of love—placing it at the highest level of importance. For Schopenhauer the "final aim of all love intrigues, be they comic or tragic, is really of more importance than all other ends in human life. What it all turns upon is nothing less than the composition of the next generation. ... It is not the weal or woe of any one individual, but that of the human race to come, which is here at stake." This view of the importance for the species of whom we choose to love was reflected in his views on [eugenics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenics "Eugenics") or good breeding. Here Schopenhauer wrote:

> With our knowledge of the complete unalterability both of character and of mental faculties, we are led to the view that a real and thorough improvement of the human race might be reached not so much from outside as from within, not so much by theory and instruction as rather by the path of generation. Plato had something of the kind in mind when, in the fifth book of his *Republic*, he explained his plan for increasing and improving his warrior caste. If we could [castrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castrate "Castrate") all scoundrels and stick all stupid geese in a convent, and give men of noble character a whole [harem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harem "Harem"), and procure men, and indeed thorough men, for all girls of intellect and understanding, then a generation would soon arise which would produce a better age than that of [Pericles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pericles "Pericles").[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89)

In another context, Schopenhauer reiterated his eugenic thesis: "If you want Utopian plans, I would say: the only solution to the problem is the [despotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despotism "Despotism") of the wise and noble members of a genuine aristocracy, a genuine nobility, achieved by [mating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mating "Mating") the most magnanimous men with the cleverest and most gifted women. This proposal constitutes my Utopia and my Platonic Republic."[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-90) Analysts (e.g., [Keith Ansell-Pearson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_Ansell-Pearson "Keith Ansell-Pearson")) have suggested that Schopenhauer's anti-[egalitarianist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism "Egalitarianism") sentiment and his support for eugenics influenced the neo-aristocratic philosophy of Friedrich Nietzsche, who initially considered Schopenhauer his mentor.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91)

#### Animal rights

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=22 "Edit section: Animal rights")]

Main article: [Arthur Schopenhauer's view on animal rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Schopenhauer%27s_view_on_animal_rights "Arthur Schopenhauer's view on animal rights")

As a consequence of his [monistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monism "Monism") philosophy, Schopenhauer was very concerned about animal welfare and rights.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92)[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PhilPapers-6) For him, all individual animals, including humans, are essentially phenomenal manifestations of the one underlying Will. For him the word "will" designates force, power, impulse, energy, and desire; it is the closest word we have that can signify both the essence of all external things and our own direct, inner experience. Since every living thing possesses will, humans and animals are fundamentally the same and can recognize themselves in each other.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93) For this reason, he claimed that a good person would have sympathy for animals, who are our fellow sufferers.

> Compassion for animals is intimately associated with goodness of character, and it may be confidently asserted that he who is cruel to living creatures cannot be a good man.
>
> — *[On the Basis of Morality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Basis_of_Morality "On the Basis of Morality")*, § 19

> Nothing leads more definitely to a recognition of the identity of the essential nature in animal and human phenomena than a study of zoology and anatomy.
>
> — *On the Basis of Morality*, chapter 8[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94)

> The assumption that animals are without rights and the illusion that our treatment of them has no moral significance is a positively outrageous example of Western crudity and barbarity. Universal compassion is the only guarantee of morality.
>
> — *On the Basis of Morality*, chapter 8[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95)

In 1841, he praised the establishment in London of the [Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_for_the_Prevention_of_Cruelty_to_Animals "Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals"), and in Philadelphia of the Animals' Friends Society. Schopenhauer went so far as to protest using the pronoun "it" in reference to animals because that led to treatment of them as though they were inanimate things.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-96) To reinforce his points, Schopenhauer referred to anecdotal reports of the look in the eyes of a monkey who had been shot[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-97) and also the grief of a baby elephant whose mother had been killed by a hunter.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-98)

Schopenhauer was very attached to his succession of pet poodles. He criticized [Spinoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baruch_Spinoza "Baruch Spinoza")'s[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99) belief that animals are a mere means for the satisfaction of humans.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100)[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101) Tim Madigan wrote that despite all of his bombast, Schopenhauer was a sympathetic character who had concerns for the suffering of animals.

> The greatest benefit conferred by the railways is that they spare millions of draught-horses their miserable existences.
>
> — *Essays and Aphorisms*, p. 171[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102)

### Intellectual interests and affinities

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=23 "Edit section: Intellectual interests and affinities")]

#### Indology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=24 "Edit section: Indology")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Schopenhauer_1852.jpg/220px-Schopenhauer_1852.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Schopenhauer_1852.jpg)

Schopenhauer, 1852

Schopenhauer read the Latin translation of the [ancient Hindu texts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_texts "Hindu texts"), the *[Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads "Upanishads")*, translated by French writer [Anquetil du Perron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Hyacinthe_Anquetil-Duperron "Abraham Hyacinthe Anquetil-Duperron")[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199768-103) from the Persian translation of Prince [Dara Shukoh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dara_Shukoh "Dara Shukoh") entitled *Sirre-Akbar* ("The Great Secret"). He was so impressed by its [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_philosophy "Indian philosophy") that he called it "the production of the highest human wisdom", and believed it contained superhuman concepts. Schopenhauer considered India as "the land of the most ancient and most pristine wisdom, the place from which [Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europeans "Europeans") could trace their descent and the tradition by which they had been influenced in so many decisive ways",[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199768-103) and regarded the *Upanishads* as "the most profitable and elevating reading which \[...] is possible in the world. It has been the solace of my life, and will be the solace of my death."[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199768-103)

Schopenhauer was first introduced to Anquetil du Perron's translation by Friedrich Majer in 1814.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke199768-103) They met during the winter of 1813–1814 in [Weimar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar "Weimar") at the home of Schopenhauer's mother, according to the biographer Safranski. Majer was a follower of [Herder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Gottfried_Herder "Johann Gottfried Herder"), and an early [Indologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indologist "Indologist"). Schopenhauer did not begin serious study of the Indic texts until the summer of 1814. Safranski maintains that, between 1815 and 1817, Schopenhauer had another important cross-pollination with Indian thought in [Dresden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dresden "Dresden"). This was through his neighbor of two years, [Karl Christian Friedrich Krause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Christian_Friedrich_Krause "Karl Christian Friedrich Krause"). Krause was then a minor and rather unorthodox philosopher who attempted to mix his own ideas with ancient Indian wisdom. Krause had also mastered [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit"), unlike Schopenhauer, and they developed a professional relationship. It was from Krause that Schopenhauer learned [meditation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meditation "Meditation") and received the closest thing to expert advice concerning Indian thought.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-104)

> The view of things \[...] that all plurality is only apparent, that in the endless series of individuals, passing simultaneously and successively into and out of life, generation after generation, age after age, there is but one and the same entity really existing, which is present and identical in all alike;—this theory, I say, was of course known long before Kant; indeed, it may be carried back to the remotest antiquity. It is the alpha and omega of the oldest book in the world, the sacred [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas "Vedas"), whose dogmatic part, or rather esoteric teaching, is found in the Upanishads. There, in almost every page this profound doctrine lies enshrined; with tireless repetition, in countless adaptations, by many varied parables and similes it is expounded and inculcated.
>
> — *On the Basis of Morality*, chapter 4[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-105)

For Schopenhauer, will had [ontological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontology "Ontology") primacy over the [intellect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intellect "Intellect"); desire is prior to thought. Schopenhauer felt this was similar to notions of [puruṣārtha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puru%E1%B9%A3%C4%81rtha "Puruṣārtha") or goals of life in [Vedānta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ved%C4%81nta "Vedānta") [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism").

In Schopenhauer's philosophy, denial of the will is attained by:

- personal experience of an extremely great suffering that leads to loss of the will to live; or
- knowledge of the essential nature of life in the world through observation of the suffering of other people.

The book *Oupnekhat* (Upanishad) always lay open on his table, and he invariably studied it before going to bed. He called the opening up of [Sanskrit literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit_literature "Sanskrit literature") "the greatest gift of our century", and predicted that the philosophy and knowledge of the Upanishads would become the cherished faith of the West.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-106) Most noticeable, in the case of Schopenhauer's work, was the significance of the *[Chandogya Upanishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandogya_Upanishad "Chandogya Upanishad")*, whose [Mahāvākya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81v%C4%81kyas "Mahāvākyas"), [Tat Tvam Asi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tat_Tvam_Asi "Tat Tvam Asi"), is mentioned throughout *The World as Will and Representation*.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-107)

#### Buddhism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=25 "Edit section: Buddhism")]

Schopenhauer noted a correspondence between his doctrines and the [Four Noble Truths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Noble_Truths "Four Noble Truths") of [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism").[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-108) Similarities centered on the principles that life involves suffering, that suffering is caused by desire ([taṇhā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%E1%B9%87h%C4%81 "Taṇhā")), and that the extinction of desire leads to liberation. Thus three of the four "truths of the Buddha" correspond to Schopenhauer's doctrine of the will.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-109) In Buddhism, while greed and lust are always unskillful, desire is ethically variable – it can be skillful, unskillful, or neutral.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-David_Burton_2004,_page_22-110)

Buddhist [nirvāṇa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nirv%C4%81%E1%B9%87a "Nirvāṇa") is not equivalent to the condition that Schopenhauer described as denial of the will. Nirvāṇa is not the extinguishing of the *person* as some Western scholars have thought, but only the "extinguishing" (the literal meaning of nirvana) of the flames of greed, hatred, and delusion that assail a person's character.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-111) Schopenhauer made the following statement in his discussion of religions:[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112)

> If I wished to take the results of my philosophy as the standard of truth, I should have to concede to Buddhism pre-eminence over the others. In any case, it must be a pleasure to me to see my doctrine in such close agreement with a religion that the majority of men on earth hold as their own, for this numbers far more followers than any other. And this agreement must be yet the more pleasing to me, inasmuch as *in my philosophizing I have certainly not been under its influence* \[emphasis added]. For up till 1818, when my work appeared, there was to be found in Europe only a very few accounts of Buddhism.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-113)

Buddhist philosopher [Keiji Nishitani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keiji_Nishitani "Keiji Nishitani") sought to distance Buddhism from Schopenhauer.[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-114) While Schopenhauer's philosophy may sound rather mystical in such a summary, his [methodology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodology "Methodology") was resolutely [empirical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical "Empirical"), rather than speculative or transcendental:

> Philosophy ... is a science, and as such has no articles of faith; accordingly, in it nothing can be assumed as existing except what is either positively given empirically, or demonstrated through indubitable conclusions.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-115)

Also note:

> This actual world of what is knowable, in which we are and which is in us, remains both the material and the limit of our consideration.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-116)

The argument that Buddhism affected Schopenhauer's philosophy more than any other [Dharmic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma "Dharma") faith loses credence since he did not begin a serious study of Buddhism until after the publication of *The World as Will and Representation* in 1818.[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-117) Scholars have started to revise earlier views about Schopenhauer's discovery of Buddhism. Proof of early interest and influence appears in Schopenhauer's 1815–16 notes (transcribed and translated by Urs App) about Buddhism. They are included in a recent case study that traces Schopenhauer's interest in Buddhism and documents its influence.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-118) Other scholarly work questions how similar Schopenhauer's philosophy actually is to Buddhism.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-119)

#### Magic and occultism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=26 "Edit section: Magic and occultism")]

Some traditions in [Western esotericism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_esotericism "Western esotericism") and [parapsychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parapsychology "Parapsychology") interested Schopenhauer and influenced his philosophical theories. He praised [animal magnetism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_magnetism "Animal magnetism") as evidence for the reality of magic in his *On the Will in Nature*, and went so far as to accept the division of magic into [left-hand and right-hand magic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-hand_path_and_right-hand_path "Left-hand path and right-hand path"), although he doubted the existence of demons.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Myth_of_Disenchantment-120)

Schopenhauer grounded magic in the Will and claimed all forms of magical transformation depended on the human Will, not on ritual. This theory notably parallels [Aleister Crowley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleister_Crowley "Aleister Crowley")'s system of magic and its emphasis on human will.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Myth_of_Disenchantment-120) Given the importance of the Will to Schopenhauer's overarching system, this amounts to "suggesting his whole philosophical system had magical powers."[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-121) Schopenhauer rejected the theory of [disenchantment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disenchantment "Disenchantment") and claimed philosophy should synthesize itself with magic, which he believed amount to "practical metaphysics".[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-122)

[Neoplatonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonism "Neoplatonism"), including the traditions of [Plotinus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plotinus "Plotinus") and to a lesser extent [Marsilio Ficino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsilio_Ficino "Marsilio Ficino"), has also been cited as an influence on Schopenhauer.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-123)

## Interests

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=27 "Edit section: Interests")]

Schopenhauer had a wide range of interests, from science and opera to occultism and literature.

In his student years, Schopenhauer went more often to lectures in the sciences than philosophy. He kept a strong interest as his personal library contained near to 200 books of scientific literature at his death, and his works refer to scientific titles not found in the library.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 170 

Many evenings were spent in the theatre, opera and ballet; Schopenhauer especially liked the operas of [Mozart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfgang_Amadeus_Mozart "Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart"), [Rossini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gioachino_Rossini "Gioachino Rossini") and [Bellini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincenzo_Bellini "Vincenzo Bellini").[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-124) Schopenhauer considered music the highest art, and played the flute during his whole life.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 30 

As a polyglot, he knew German, Italian, Spanish, French, English, [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin "Latin") and [ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek "Ancient Greek"), and was an avid reader of poetry and literature. He particularly revered [Goethe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_von_Goethe "Johann Wolfgang von Goethe"), [Petrarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrarch "Petrarch"), [Calderón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Calder%C3%B3n_de_la_Barca "Pedro Calderón de la Barca") and [Shakespeare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Shakespeare "William Shakespeare").

> If Goethe had not been sent into the world simultaneously with Kant in order to counterbalance him, so to speak, in the spirit of the age, the latter would have been haunted like a nightmare many an aspiring mind and would have oppressed it with great affliction. But now the two have an infinitely wholesome effect from opposite directions and will probably raise the German spirit to a height surpassing even that of antiquity.[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cartwright-16): 240 

In philosophy, his most important influences were, according to himself, Kant, [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato") and the [Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads "Upanishads"). Concerning the Upanishads and [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas "Vedas"), he writes in *The World as Will and Representation*:

> If the reader has also received the benefit of the Vedas, the access to which by means of the Upanishads is in my eyes the greatest privilege which this still young century (1818) may claim before all previous centuries, if then the reader, I say, has received his initiation in primeval Indian wisdom, and received it with an open heart, he will be prepared in the very best way for hearing what I have to tell him. It will not sound to him strange, as to many others, much less disagreeable; for I might, if it did not sound conceited, contend that every one of the detached statements which constitute the Upanishads, may be deduced as a necessary result from the fundamental thoughts which I have to enunciate, though those deductions themselves are by no means to be found there.[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-125)

## Thoughts on other philosophers

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=28 "Edit section: Thoughts on other philosophers")]

### Giordano Bruno and Spinoza

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Giordano Bruno and Spinoza")]

Schopenhauer saw [Bruno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giordano_Bruno "Giordano Bruno") and [Spinoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baruch_Spinoza "Baruch Spinoza") as philosophers not bound to their age or nation. "Both were fulfilled by the thought, that as manifold the appearances of the world may be, it is still *one* being, that appears in all of them. ... Consequently, there is no place for God as creator of the world in their philosophy, but God is the world itself."[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Spinoza_and_Bruno-126)[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Presentation-127)

Schopenhauer expressed regret that Spinoza stuck, for the presentation of his philosophy, with the concepts of [scholasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism "Scholasticism") and [Cartesian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartesian_philosophy "Cartesian philosophy"), and tried to use geometrical proofs that do not hold because of vague and overly broad definitions. Bruno on the other hand, who knew much about nature and ancient literature, presented his ideas with Italian vividness, and is amongst philosophers the only one who comes near Plato's poetic and dramatic power of exposition.[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Spinoza_and_Bruno-126)[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Presentation-127)

Schopenhauer noted that their philosophies do not provide any ethics, and it is therefore very remarkable that Spinoza called his main work *[Ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics_\(Spinoza_book\) "Ethics (Spinoza book)")*. In fact, it could be considered complete from the standpoint of life-affirmation, if one completely ignores morality and self-denial.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-128) It is yet even more remarkable that Schopenhauer mentions Spinoza as an example of the denial of the will, if one uses the French biography by Jean Maximilien Lucas[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-129) as the key to *[Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tractatus_de_Intellectus_Emendatione "Tractatus de Intellectus Emendatione")*.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-130)

### Immanuel Kant

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Immanuel Kant")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Immanuel_Kant_portrait_c1790.jpg/220px-Immanuel_Kant_portrait_c1790.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Immanuel_Kant_portrait_c1790.jpg)

Schopenhauer's philosophy took Kant's work as its foundation. While he praised Kant's greatness, he nonetheless included a highly detailed criticism of Kantian philosophy as an appendix to *The World as Will and Representation*.

See also: [Critique of the Kantian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critique_of_the_Kantian_philosophy "Critique of the Kantian philosophy") and [Schopenhauer's criticism of Kant's schemata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schopenhauer%27s_criticism_of_Kant%27s_schemata "Schopenhauer's criticism of Kant's schemata")

Kant's influence on Schopenhauer's development, personally as well as in philosophy, was extensive. Kant's philosophy lies at the foundation of Schopenhauer's, and he had high praise for the [Transcendental Aesthetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critique_of_Pure_Reason#Transcendental_Aesthetic "Critique of Pure Reason") section of Kant's *Critique of Pure Reason*. Schopenhauer maintained that Kant stands in the same relation to philosophers such as Berkeley and [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato"), as Copernicus to [Hicetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hicetas "Hicetas"), [Philolaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philolaus "Philolaus"), and [Aristarchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristarchus_of_Samos "Aristarchus of Samos"): Kant succeeded in demonstrating what previous philosophers merely asserted.

Schopenhauer writes about Kant's influence on his work in the preface to the second edition of *The World as Will and Representation*:

> I have already explained in the preface to the first edition, that my philosophy is founded on that of Kant, and therefore presupposes a thorough knowledge of it. I repeat this here. For Kant's teaching produces in the mind of everyone who has comprehended it a fundamental change which is so great that it may be regarded as an intellectual new-birth. It alone is able really to remove the inborn realism which proceeds from the original character of the intellect, which neither [Berkeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Berkeley "George Berkeley") nor [Malebranche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Malebranche "Nicolas Malebranche") succeed in doing, for they remain too much in the universal, while Kant goes into the particular, and indeed in a way that is quite unexampled both before and after him, and which has quite a peculiar, and, we might say, immediate effect upon the mind in consequence of which it undergoes a complete undeception, and forthwith looks at all things in another light. Only in this way can any one become susceptible to the more positive expositions which I have to give. On the other hand, he who has not mastered the Kantian philosophy, whatever else he may have studied, is, as it were, in a state of innocence; that is to say, he remains in the grasp of that natural and childish realism in which we are all born, and which fits us for everything possible, with the single exception of philosophy.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-131)

In his study room, one bust was of [Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha "Gautama Buddha"), the other was of Kant.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-132) The bond which Schopenhauer felt with the philosopher of Königsberg is demonstrated in an unfinished poem he dedicated to Kant (included in volume 2 of the *Parerga*):

> With my eyes I followed thee into the blue sky,\
> And there thy flight dissolved from view.\
> Alone I stayed in the crowd below,\
> Thy word and thy book my only solace.—\
> Through the strains of thy inspiring words\
> I sought to dispel the dreary solitude.\
> Strangers on all sides surround me.\
> The world is desolate and life interminable.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-133)

Schopenhauer dedicated one fifth of his main work, *The World as Will and Representation*, to a detailed [criticism of the Kantian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critique_of_the_Kantian_philosophy "Critique of the Kantian philosophy").

Schopenhauer praised Kant for his distinction between appearance and the [thing-in-itself](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thing-in-itself "Thing-in-itself"), whereas the general consensus in [German idealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_idealism "German idealism") was that this was the weakest spot of Kant's theory,[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-42) since, according to Kant, causality can find application on objects of experience only, and consequently, things-in-themselves cannot be the cause of appearances. The inadmissibility of this reasoning was also acknowledged by Schopenhauer. He insisted that this was a true conclusion, drawn from false premises.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-134)

### Post-Kantian school

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=31 "Edit section: Post-Kantian school")]

The leading figures of [post-Kantian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_idealism "German idealism")—[Johann Gottlieb Fichte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Gottlieb_Fichte "Johann Gottlieb Fichte"), [F. W. J. Schelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Wilhelm_Joseph_Schelling "Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling") and [G. W. F. Hegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Friedrich_Hegel "Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel")—were not respected by Schopenhauer. He argued that they were not philosophers at all, for they lacked "the first requirement of a philosopher, namely a seriousness and honesty of inquiry."[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-135) Rather, they were merely sophists who, excelling in the art of beguiling the public, pursued their own selfish interests (such as professional advancement within the university system). Diatribes against the alleged vacuity, dishonesty, pomposity, and self-interest of these contemporaries are to be found throughout Schopenhauer's published writings. The following passage is an example:

> All this explains the painful impression with which we are seized when, after studying genuine thinkers, we come to the writings of Fichte and Schelling, or even to the presumptuously scribbled nonsense of Hegel, produced as it was with a boundless, though justified, confidence in German stupidity. With those genuine thinkers one always found an *honest* investigation of truth and just as *honest* an attempt to communicate their ideas to others. Therefore whoever reads Kant, Locke, Hume, Malebranche, Spinoza, and Descartes feels elevated and agreeably impressed. This is produced through communion with a noble mind which has and awakens ideas and which thinks and sets one thinking. The reverse of all this takes place when we read the above-mentioned three German sophists. An unbiased reader, opening one of their books and then asking himself whether this is the tone of a thinker wanting to instruct or that of a charlatan wanting to impress, cannot be five minutes in any doubt; here everything breathes so much of *dishonesty*.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-136)

Schopenhauer deemed Schelling the most talented of the three and wrote that he would recommend his "elucidatory paraphrase of the highly important doctrine of Kant" concerning the intelligible character, if he had been honest enough to admit he was parroting Kant, instead of hiding this relation in a cunning manner.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-137)

Schopenhauer reserved his most unqualified damning condemnation for Hegel, whom he considered less worthy than Fichte or Schelling. Whereas Fichte was merely a windbag (*Windbeutel*), Hegel was a "commonplace, inane, loathsome, repulsive, and ignorant charlatan."[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-138) The philosophers [Karl Popper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Popper "Karl Popper") and [Mario Bunge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Bunge "Mario Bunge") agreed with this distinction.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-139)[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Bunge1-140) Hegel, Schopenhauer wrote in the preface to his *Two Fundamental Problems of Ethics*, not only "performed no service to philosophy, but he has had a detrimental influence on philosophy, and thereby on German literature in general, really a downright stupefying, or we could even say a pestilential influence, which it is therefore the duty of everyone capable of thinking for himself and judging for himself to counteract in the most express terms at every opportunity."[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-141)

## Influence and legacy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Schopenhauer\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Influence and legacy")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Arthur_Schopenhauer_by_Elisabet_Ney.jpg/220px-Arthur_Schopenhauer_by_Elisabet_Ney.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arthur_Schopenhauer_by_Elisabet_Ney.jpg)

[Sculpture of Schopenhauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Schopenhauer_\(sculpture\) "Arthur Schopenhauer (sculpture)") by [Elisabeth Ney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisabeth_Ney "Elisabeth Ney")

Schopenhauer remained the most influential German philosopher until the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War "First World War").[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Weltschmerz-142) His philosophy was a starting point for a new generation of philosophers including [Julius Bahnsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Bahnsen "Julius Bahnsen"), [Paul Deussen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Deussen "Paul Deussen"), Lazar von Hellenbach, [Karl Robert Eduard von Hartmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Robert_Eduard_von_Hartmann "Karl Robert Eduard von Hartmann"), Ernst Otto Lindner, [Philipp Mainländer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philipp_Mainl%C3%A4nder "Philipp Mainländer"), [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche "Friedrich Nietzsche"), [Olga Plümacher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olga_Pl%C3%BCmacher "Olga Plümacher") and [Agnes Taubert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnes_Taubert "Agnes Taubert"). His legacy shaped the intellectual debate, and forced movements that were utterly opposed to him, [neo-Kantianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Kantianism "Neo-Kantianism") and [positivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positivism "Positivism"), to address issues they would otherwise have completely ignored, and in doing so he changed them markedly.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Weltschmerz-142) The French writer [Maupassant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_de_Maupassant "Guy de Maupassant") commented that "to-day even those who execrate him seem to carry in their own souls particles of his thought".[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-143) Other philosophers of the 19th century who cited his influence include [Hans Vaihinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Vaihinger "Hans Vaihinger"), [Volkelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Volkelt "Johannes Volkelt"), [Solovyov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Solovyov_\(philosopher\) "Vladimir Solovyov (philosopher)") and [Weininger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Weininger "Otto Weininger").

Schopenhauer was well read by physicists, most notably [Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein"), [Schrödinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erwin_Schr%C3%B6dinger "Erwin Schrödinger"), [Wolfgang Pauli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfgang_Pauli "Wolfgang Pauli"),[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-144) and [Majorana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ettore_Majorana "Ettore Majorana").[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Majorana-145) Einstein described Schopenhauer's thoughts as a "continual consolation" and called him a genius.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-146) In his Berlin study three figures hung on the wall: [Faraday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Faraday "Michael Faraday"), [Maxwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Clerk_Maxwell "James Clerk Maxwell"), Schopenhauer.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-147) [Konrad Wachsmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_Wachsmann "Konrad Wachsmann") recalled: "He often sat with one of the well-worn Schopenhauer volumes, and as he sat there, he seemed so pleased, as if he were engaged with a serene and cheerful work."[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-148)

When [Erwin Schrödinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erwin_Schr%C3%B6dinger "Erwin Schrödinger") discovered Schopenhauer ("the greatest savant of the West") he considered switching his study of physics to philosophy.[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-149) He maintained the idealistic views during the rest of his life.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-150) [Wolfgang Pauli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfgang_Pauli "Wolfgang Pauli") accepted the main tenet of Schopenhauer's metaphysics, that the [thing-in-itself](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thing-in-itself "Thing-in-itself") is will.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-151)

But most of all Schopenhauer is famous for his influence on artists. [Richard Wagner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Wagner "Richard Wagner") became one of the earliest and most famous adherents of the Schopenhauerian philosophy.[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-152) The admiration was not mutual, and Schopenhauer proclaimed: "I remain faithful to Rossini and Mozart!"[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-153) So he has been [nicknamed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nicknames_of_philosophers "List of nicknames of philosophers") "the artist's philosopher".[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-iep-1) See also [Influence of Schopenhauer on *Tristan und Isolde*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tristan_und_Isolde#Influence_of_Schopenhauer_on_Tristan_und_Isolde "Tristan und Isolde").

[![Schopenhauer depicted on a 500 million Danzig papiermark note (1923)](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/DAN-28a-Danzig-500MIL_Mark_%281923%29.jpg/235px-DAN-28a-Danzig-500MIL_Mark_%281923%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DAN-28a-Danzig-500MIL_Mark_\(1923\).jpg)

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DAN-28a-Danzig-500MIL_Mark_\(1923\).jpg "File:DAN-28a-Danzig-500MIL Mark (1923).jpg")

Schopenhauer depicted on a 500 million Danzig [papiermark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papiermark#Danzig "Papiermark") note (1923)

Under the influence of Schopenhauer, [Leo Tolstoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Tolstoy "Leo Tolstoy") became convinced that the truth of all religions lies in self-renunciation. When he read Schopenhauer's philosophy, Tolstoy exclaimed "at present I am convinced that Schopenhauer is the greatest genius among men. ... It is the whole world in an incomparably beautiful and clear reflection."[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-154) He said that what he has written in *[War and Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_and_Peace "War and Peace")* is also said by Schopenhauer in *The World as Will and Representation*.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-155)

[Jorge Luis Borges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Luis_Borges "Jorge Luis Borges") remarked that the reason he had never attempted to write a systematic account of his world view, despite his penchant for philosophy and metaphysics in particular, was because Schopenhauer had already written it for him.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-156)

Other figures in literature who were strongly influenced by Schopenhauer were [Thomas Mann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Mann "Thomas Mann"), [Thomas Hardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hardy "Thomas Hardy"), [Afanasy Fet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afanasy_Fet "Afanasy Fet"), [J.-K. Huysmans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joris-Karl_Huysmans "Joris-Karl Huysmans") and [George Santayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Santayana "George Santayana").[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-157) In [Herman Melville's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Melville "Herman Melville") final years, while he wrote *[Billy Budd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Budd "Billy Budd")*, he read Schopenhauer's essays and marked them heavily. Scholar Brian Yothers notes that Melville "marked numerous misanthropic and even suicidal remarks, suggesting an attraction to the most extreme sorts of solitude, but he also made note of Schopenhauer's reflection on the moral ambiguities of genius."[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-158) Schopenhauer's attraction to and discussions of both Eastern and Western religions in conjunction with each other made an impression on Melville in his final years.

[Sergei Prokofiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Prokofiev "Sergei Prokofiev"), although initially reluctant to engage with works noted for their pessimism, became fascinated with Schopenhauer after reading *Aphorisms on the Wisdom of Life* in *Parerga and Paralipomena*. "With his truths Schopenhauer gave me a spiritual world and an awareness of happiness."[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-159)

[Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche "Friedrich Nietzsche") owed the awakening of his philosophical interest to reading *The World as Will and Representation* and admitted that he was one of the few philosophers that he respected, dedicating to him his essay "Schopenhauer als Erzieher",[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-160) one of his *[Untimely Meditations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untimely_Meditations "Untimely Meditations")*.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/DBP_1988_1357_Arthur_Schopenhauer.jpg/220px-DBP_1988_1357_Arthur_Schopenhauer.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DBP_1988_1357_Arthur_Schopenhauer.jpg)

Commemorative stamp of the Deutsche Bundespost

Early in his career, [Ludwig Wittgenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_Wittgenstein "Ludwig Wittgenstein") adopted Schopenhauer's epistemological idealism, and some traits of Schopenhauer's influence (particularly Schopenhauerian transcendentalism) can be observed in the *[Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tractatus_Logico-Philosophicus "Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus")*.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-161)[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-162) Later on, Wittgenstein rejected epistemological [transcendental idealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcendental_idealism "Transcendental idealism") for [Gottlob Frege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottlob_Frege "Gottlob Frege")'s conceptual [realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysical_realism "Metaphysical realism"). In later years, Wittgenstein became highly dismissive of Schopenhauer, describing him as an ultimately shallow thinker.[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Culture_&_Value,_p.24,_1933%E2%80%934-163)[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-164) His friend [Bertrand Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_Russell "Bertrand Russell") had a low opinion on the philosopher, and even came to attack him in his [*History of Western Philosophy*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Western_Philosophy_\(Russell\) "History of Western Philosophy (Russell)") for hypocritically praising asceticism yet not acting upon it.[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-165)

Opposite to Russell on the foundations of mathematics, the Dutch mathematician [L. E. J. Brouwer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._E._J._Brouwer "L. E. J. Brouwer") incorporated Kant's and Schopenhauer's ideas in the philosophical school of [intuitionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intuitionism "Intuitionism"), where mathematics is considered as a purely mental activity instead of an analytic activity wherein objective properties of reality are revealed. Brouwer was also influenced by Schopenhauer's metaphysics, and wrote an essay on mysticism.

Schopenhauer's philosophy has made its way into a novel, *[The Schopenhauer Cure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Schopenhauer_Cure "The Schopenhauer Cure")*, by American existential psychiatrist and emeritus professor of psychiatry [Irvin Yalom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irvin_Yalom "Irvin Yalom").

Schopenhauer's philosophy, and the discussions on [philosophical pessimism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_pessimism "Philosophical pessimism") it has engendered, has been the focus of contemporary thinkers such as [David Benatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Benatar "David Benatar"), [Thomas Ligotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Ligotti "Thomas Ligotti"), and [Eugene Thacker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugene_Thacker "Eugene Thacker"). Their work also served as an inspiration for the popular HBO TV series *[True Detective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_Detective "True Detective")* as well as *[Life Is Beautiful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_Is_Beautiful "Life Is Beautiful")*.[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-166) In this regard, Schopenhauer is sometimes considered the founding father of today's [antinatalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antinatalism "Antinatalism").[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-167)

Advocates of [idealism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysical_idealism "Metaphysical idealism") in contemporary [analytic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytic_philosophy "Analytic philosophy") and [neuroscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuroscience "Neuroscience") such as [Bernardo Kastrup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardo_Kastrup "Bernardo Kastrup") and [Christof Koch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christof_Koch "Christof Koch") owe their philosophical system, in part, to the metaphysics of Schopenhauer. [\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-168)[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-169)

5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-5)** Lennart Svensson, *Borderline: A Traditionalist Outlook for Modern Man*, Numen Books, 2015, p. 71: "\[Schopenhauer] said that 'the world is our conception'. A world without a perceiver would in that case be an impossibility. But we can—he said—gain knowledge about Essential Reality for looking into ourselves, by introspection. ... This is one of many examples of the anthropic principle. The world is there for the sake of man."

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-0-521-04406-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-521-04406-6 "Special:BookSources/978-0-521-04406-6"). "For Kant, the mathematical sublime, as seen for example in the starry heavens, suggests to imagination the infinite, which in turn leads by subtle turns of contemplation to the concept of God. Schopenhauer's atheism will have none of this, and he rightly observes that despite adopting Kant's distinction between the dynamical and mathematical sublime, his theory of the sublime, making reference to the struggles and sufferings of struggles and sufferings of Will, is unlike Kant's."

"The contrary doctrine that the law of causality results from experience, which was the scepticism of Hume, is first refuted by this. For the independence of the knowledge of causality of all experience,—that is, its a priori character—can only be deduced from the dependence of all experience upon it; and this deduction can only be accomplished by proving, in the manner here indicated, and explained in the passages referred to above, that the knowledge of causality is included in perception in general, to which all experience belongs, and therefore in respect of experience is completely a priori, does not presuppose it, but is presupposed by it as a condition."

55. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-55)** This comment by Schopenhauer was called "an acute observation" by [Sir Thomas L. Heath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T._L._Heath "T. L. Heath"). In his translation of [The Elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euclid%27s_Elements "Euclid's Elements"), vol. 1, Book I, "Note on Common Notion 4", Heath made this judgment and also noted that Schopenhauer's remark "was a criticism in advance of [Helmholtz'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_von_Helmholtz "Hermann von Helmholtz") theory". Helmholtz had "maintained that geometry requires us to assume the actual existence of rigid bodies and their free mobility in space" and is therefore "dependent on mechanics".

97. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-97)** "I recall having read of an Englishman who, while hunting in India, had shot a monkey; he could not forget the look which the dying animal gave him, and since then had never again fired at monkeys." *On the Basis of Morality*, § 19.
98. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-98)** "\[Sir William Harris] describes how he shot his first elephant, a female. The next morning he went to look for the dead animal; all the other elephants had fled from the neighborhood except a young one, who had spent the night with its dead mother. Forgetting all fear, he came toward the sportsmen with the clearest and liveliest evidence of inconsolable grief, and put his tiny trunk round them in order to appeal to them for help. Harris says he was then filled with real remorse for what he had done, and felt as if he had committed a murder." *On the basis of morality*, § 19.

100. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-100)** Spinoza, *Ethics*, Pt. IV, Prop. XXXVII, Note I.: "Still I do not deny that beasts feel: what I deny is, that we may not consult our own advantage and use them as we please, treating them in a way which best suits us; for their nature is not like ours ..." This is the exact opposite of Schopenhauer's doctrine. Also, *Ethics*, Appendix, 26, "whatsoever there be in nature beside man, a regard for our advantage does not call on us to preserve, but to preserve or destroy according to its various capacities, and to adapt to our use as best we may."
101. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-101)** "Such are the matters which I engage to prove in Prop. xviii of this Part, whereby it is plain that the law against the slaughtering of animals is founded rather on vain superstition and womanish pity than on sound reason. The rational quest of what is useful to us further teaches us the necessity of associating ourselves with our fellow-men, but not with beasts, or things, whose nature is different from our own; we have the same rights in respect to them as they have in respect to us. Nay, as everyone's right is defined by his virtue, or power, men have far greater rights over beasts than beasts have over men. Still I affirm that beasts feel. But I also affirm that we may consult our own advantage and use them as we please, treating them in the way which best suits us; for their nature is not like ours, and their emotions are naturally different from human emotions." *Ethics*, Part 4, Prop. 37, Note 1.

112. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-112)** "Schopenhauer is often said to be the first modern Western philosopher to attempt integration of his work with Eastern ways of thinking. That he was the first is true, but the claim that he was *influenced* by Indian thought needs qualification. There is a remarkable correspondence in broad terms between some central Schopenhauerian doctrines and Buddhism: notably in the views that empirical existence is suffering, that suffering originates in desires, and that salvation can be attained by the extinction of desires. These three 'truths of the Buddha' are mirrored closely in the essential structure of the doctrine of the will." (On this, see Dorothea W. Dauer, *Schopenhauer as Transmitter of Buddhist Ideas*. Note also the discussion by Bryan Magee, *The Philosophy of Schopenhauer*, pp. 14–15, 316–321). Janaway, Christopher, *Self and World in Schopenhauer's Philosophy*, p. 28 f.

"Arthur Schopenhauer was the most famous and influential philosopher in Germany from 1860 until the First World War. ... Schopenhauer had a profound influence on two intellectual movements of the late 19th century that were utterly opposed to him: neo-Kantianism and positivism. 154. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-154)** Tolstoy's letter to Afanasy Fet on 30 August 1869. "Do you know what this summer has meant for me? Constant raptures over Schopenhauer and a whole series of spiritual delights as I've never experienced before. I have brought all of his works and read him over and over, Kant too by the way. Assuredly no student has ever learned and discovered so much in one semester as I have during this summer. I do not know if I shall ever change my opinion, but at present I am convinced that Schopenhauer is the greatest genius among men. You say he is so-so, he has written a few things on philosophy? What is so-so? It is the whole world in an incomparably beautiful and clear reflection. I have started to translate him. Won't you help me? Indeed, I cannot understand how his name can be unknown. The only explanation for this can only be the one he so often repeats, that is, that there is scarcely anyone but idiots in the world."
